THIS first entry in Significant Ships from one of
Korea's smaller shipyards, based in the south-coast port of Tongyoung, is one of three sister ships ordered by Qatar Shipping from a standard 37,000dwt chemical/product tanker design developed in Shina’s own technical departments, and which has already attracted a substantial number of orders from various owners. Alnoman is a single-decked vessel with a forecastle which is linked to the deckhouse aft by means of a fore and aft gangway built above the deck. To suit length restrictions in force in many European and American terminal ports, Shina has opted to base its design on a 175.90m overall length, whilst, at the same time ensuring Panama Canal navigation with a beam of 31.00m. Alnoman has been constructed with less than 30% high-tensile steel and complies with the latest international rules and regulations, including Exxon requirements. Amongst technical equipment on board is a voyage data recorder. Cargoes classified as IMO
Type III can be carried in a cargo space contained within a double-skin hull, divided by a centreline, and six transverse bulkheads, into 12 cargo tanks, two slop tanks and one residuals tank. All are finished with a phenol-epoxy coating applied to a plain surface, since all steelwork stiffening, including deck beams, is fitted outside the tanks. The Type III range of cargoes includes caustic soda and molasses, and the Frank Mohn, hydraulically operated, submerged pumps fitted into each tank, together with pipework and fittings, are of stainless steel construction to suit these products. Cargo is handled through midship manifolds port and starboard, served by a 10tonne swl, electro-hydraulic hose- handling crane, with discharge also possible over the stern by means of a 300mm line. The main engine is an MAN B&W 6S50MC unit built by HSD, having an MCR output of 8580kW at 127rev/min and driving a four-bladed propeller of 5750mm diameter. Despite having a block coefficient of around 0.80, this class of tanker is designed for a service speed of 15knots at 90% MCR and with a 15% sea margin. Electrical supply comes from three 740kW
Taiyo alternators fitted with STX (Ssangyong) diesel prime movers, and steam requirements are served by an
Aalborg oil-fired boiler. Unusually for this type of vessel, a 'flap-type' rudder is fitted, with manoeuvring further enhanced by a Kawasaki 950kW bow thruster. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

Illustration shows 8234-class prototype Norasia
Valparaiso
DESPITE the economic difficulties of the past year, the Gdynia Shipyard managed to keep its building programme moving to some degree, with the result that after several months delay, it was finally possible to resume construction of what are understood to be the largest and fastest container ships yet built in Poland, with a capacity of 4444TEU. Based on an earlier design for a 4000TEU vessel, this 8234 series has been upgraded to suit the owner's requirements for stability intake and sustained speed, by increasing ship length and fitting a more powerful engine. A new hull form was tested at the Gdansk
Model Basin, with particular attention given to propeller design, shape of rudder (which includes the fitting of a bulb), and arrangement and size of bilge keels. This also presents an almost completely rectangular upper deck - narrowed only at the bow. The double-skin hull encloses seven cargo holds forward of the machinery room, with one small hold under the deck aft, upon which three FEU stacks can be stowed. The hatches are closed by weathertight pontoon covers of open construction, and their longitudinal disposition allows wider spacing between container bays than in other, competitive designs, facilitating lashing access and providing space for lashing bridges. Electrical connections are provided on deck for 450 refrigerated FEU to be carried, with arrangements made for the future installation of additional ventilation in the holds if optional reefer positions are utilised. The stability characteristics of the design have been enhanced by optimised tank subdivision, with the double bottom arrangement laid out without a central duct-keel. The fore and aft access passages, normally found in the 'tweendeck' above the side tanks in this type of vessel, have been eliminated, and the tanks extended to upper deck level to improve damage stability. An eight-tier deckhouse is positioned above the upper deck, providing a line-of-sight over the maximum seven tiers of containers which can be loaded. Its height also ensures that an owner's requirement for a 51.80m air draught above keel can be maintained. For this reason also, the signal mast above the bridge is arranged to fold. Korean builder Hyundai Heavy Industries supplied the Sulzer 8RTA96C main engine, which develops 43,920kW at 100rev/min, to obtain a service speed of 24.90knots at 90% full power and allowing 15% sea margin, from the FP propeller, designed and manufactured by Stone Manganese Marine. The four alternator sets, two each of 2250kVA and 2000kVA rating, have been supplied by A van Kaick, and are fitted with MAN B&W prime movers, with control by a power management system, which forms part of an automation and control package designed by STN Atlas
Marine (SAM) Electronics. Control of engineroom equipment is carried out from a ship operating centre in the deckhouse, using an integrated automation system (IAS) performing all alarm, control and monitoring functions. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

AMARANTA: Poland's largest box-ship

ARKLOW River forms part of a new class of circa- 4500dwt vessels currently being delivered from the Barkmeijer yard, and built to a design which shares many features with two sister ships completed in 2002 by fellow Conoship member, Shipyard Bijlsma (Arklow
Rally: Significant Ships of 2002). Although of similar dimensions to those vessels, Arklow River does differ by having specific arrangements included for the carriage of containers, with her tanktop increased in thickness to 18mm, and strengthened to allow a maximum permissable loading of 15tonnes/m2 and container point stack loading of 42tonnes. The single 62.40m x 11.50m box-shaped hold extends into the 2.31m high hatch coaming, and can accommodate 102TEU, stowed in three tiers and four rows, with 48TEU carried on the pontoon hatch covers in four rows, two tiers high at the bridge front reducing to single tier forward. Two corrugated-steel, single-wall, moveable bulkheads provide portable cargo divisions which are positioned at the top by means of slots in the hatch coaming, and at the bottom using the tanktop container fittings. These can be variously arranged to suit grain and other bulk cargoes which, along with steel coils, are a major component of Arklow's trading pattern. Additionally, lashing eyes are arranged in the hold sides to suit project cargoes. The hatch covers and the moveable bulkheads are handled by a travelling crane which runs along the hatch coaming and stows at the bridge front when not in use. Facilities are included for the carriage of dangerous cargoes in accordance with
SOLAS chapter II-2 Regulation 54, and dehumidifying and mechanical hold ventilation arrangements are fitted. The hull form incorporates a radiused transom, and features a full aft body which is of particular interest for the two fully integrated tunnels, port and starboard, aligned from the bilge-strakes around frame 30, to the centreline of the vessel. These intersect just above the propeller and improve water flow to it, particularly in shallow water, and maintain propeller immersion, even in difficult weather conditions. The arrangement has been adopted by the builder in numerous designs and when fitted in conjunction with a flap-type rudder is said to give excellent manoeuvring performance. The double hull structure contains separate side and bottom tanks, with the latter divided by a centreline duct keel carrying the main piping systems. These are served by two ballast pumps, two general service pumps (one of which is fitted forward in the bow thruster room), and three ejectors. Poop and forecastle erections are included, and a breakwater behind the latter gives protection to containers. Accommodation is arranged aft for eight persons, occupying three decks and featuring bulkheads and ceilings in accordance with
SOLAS fire protection method I-C; A-60, A-30 and
A-0 insulation; and floating floors and wainscotting. The main engine is the same as that fitted in Arklow
Rally: namely an MaK 6M25 unit developing 1800kW at 750rev/min, but driving the high-skew CP propeller at a higher 197rev/min, via a reduction gearbox (both gearbox and propeller are provided by Scana Volda under the latter company's package agreement with
MaK). A PTO connects with a 435kVA alternator, and two 175kVA diesel-driven alternators are also fitted, one of which operates in emergency/harbour mode and is positioned outside the engineroom in the superstructure. A 250kW thruster is installed forward. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

THIS ro-ro ferry has been purpose-built to serve the regular coastal freight routes operating between the
Spanish mainland and the Balearic Islands, and, in particular, by reducing journey time to just six hours, to allow a daily round-trip to be completed between
Barcelona and Palma (Majorca). The vessel is laid out with machinery aft, below the main deck, and the navigating bridge and accommodation in a superstructure, located in the forward third, which also includes an open-ended garage built over the fore end of the upper deck (No 5). Vehicles enter at main deck (No 3) level over a stern door/ramp, formed of two sections designed to accept loadings up to 78tonnes. The port section is 14m long x 11m wide: starboard, 14m x 6m, with each section having 2m-long end flaps. A fixed ramp, accepting loadings of 44tonnes, links with the upper deck (No 5), where the open section aft is available for the carriage of hazardous cargoes, and another connects with the cargo hold, arranged at tanktop level. A hinged cover on the main deck seals this access. Clear heights of around 4.75m in the hold and garage spaces allow trailers to be readily accommodated, however, an increase to 7m between main and upper decks not only permits two tiers of containers, loaded on trailers, to be carried, but allows a hoistable car-deck (No 4), served by a 'crocodile' ramp, to be fitted at the forward end of this space. Space is provided beneath, when the ramp is in use, to accept trailers. A cargo control office is located at the aft end of the main deck, and a cargo computer is fitted to check the loading arrangements and ensure stability and strength safety limits are not exceeded. Decks 7, 8 and 9 in the superstructure provide accommodation for 120 passengers (mainly trailer drivers) and 20 crew, with a spacious wheelhouse above on deck 10. Deck 7 offers 29, four-berth passenger cabins, plus 2 x two-berth rooms for disabled travellers, a TV room, and a sitting room, separated from single- berth crew cabins in the forward area, by the reception hall and office. Officers and crew messroom/lounges, and a self-service cafeteria, boutiques and observation lounge for passengers, are arranged on deck 8, adjacent to the galley and provision storerooms, and officers are berthed on deck 9. The machinery installation is based upon two Wärtsilä 12V46C main engines, each developing 12,600kW MCR at 500rev/min and arranged to burn IFO 380 heavy fuel with reduced NOx emissions. The drive to the twin
CP propellers, positioned on extended shafts forward of the twin rudders, and outboard of a central skeg, is through two Reintjes reduction gearboxes, each having an 800kW power take-off to a Stamford 1000kVA alternator. Two 900kVA diesel-alternator sets are also included. Vulkan highly flexible couplings link the main engines to the gearboxes, also the gearboxes to the shaft alternators, and auxiliary engines to their alternators. The installation is served by a central cooling system, and an integrated automation system with more than 1200 hardware input and output channels, and 1600 internal monitoring channels, provides information on the ship's more important facilities and ensures correct ship operation. THE leading positions held in the LNG market by
Norwegian owner Bergesen, and shipbuilder
Daewoo, is demonstrated by their combined involvement in the construction of Berge Everett, another example of the Korean company's circa 140,000m3 capacity, specialist Type 2G gas carrier design, introduced for worldwide trading in a rapidly expanding fuel distribution network. A number of factors required consideration in the development of this class of vessel, many of which relate to parameters existing at loading and discharging terminals, where positions of mooring equipment, fenders, gangway and manifolds are of particular importance. In addition, in order to ensure an even keel ballast arrival condition with peak tanks empty, and allowing consumables at 50% maximum capacity and three days reserve, the disposition and quantity of water ballast carried has necessitated careful study. The layout is also arranged to enable the vessel to discharge at the Everett (Boston, USA) terminal where an air draught restriction of 41.2m from the ballast waterline is enforced. Within a double-hull structure there are four cargo tanks separated by cofferdams, and which extend above the upper deck to form a trunk. These are constructed to the Gaz Transport NO96 E-2 membrane system which uses Invar stainless steel sheet with a nickel content of 36%, for primary and secondary barriers, with perlite granules in plywood boxes inserted between them, and between the secondary barrier and the shell. This system, together with hull scantlings and cargo pumps, is suitable for the carriage of cargo of maximum specific gravity 0.50 at -163oC, and maximum boil-off rate 0.15% cargo volume. Underdeck passageways in the trunk deck space provide fore and aft access, and a duct keel in the double bottom carries pipes and cables. Cargo handling is by means of eight Ebara, aluminium alloy, submerged, centrifugal electric pumps, each with a duty of 1700m3/h and together capable of discharging a full cargo in 12 hours. Other equipment forming part of the cargo system includes high- and low-duty compressors, main and forcing vaporisers, and boil-off/warm-up heaters. Heavy-fuel tanks are contained within the double hull and have a capacity for 13,000 nautical miles steaming at 90% MCR, allowing five days margin without the use of boil-off gas. Although Daewoo is looking at the use of alternatives to the 'conventional' steam turbine propulsion system, it is this which has been adopted for Berge Everett, in the form of a Kawasaki cross-compound unit developing 36,690shp (26,985kW) at 88rev/min. This drives a five- bladed, FP propeller for a service speed of 19.50knots at 90% MCR. Two Mitsubishi steam-turbine-driven, 3450kW alternators are the main source of electrical power, supplemented by a Rolls-Royce/Bergen-driven diesel-alternator set of similar output. The two boilers are arranged for dual-fuel burning, and each produce 56tonnes/h. Brunvoll supplied the 1600kW bow thruster which provides manoeuvring aid to the semi- balanced rudder, fitted in an open-water sternframe. Accommodation is arranged for 43 persons in the main superstructure aft, with the living quarters completely separate from the engine and funnel casings. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

VOLHARDING is currently operating from three sites in the northern part of The Netherlands. Waterhuizen provides head-office and design facilities, and Foxhol fabricates modules and accommodation blocks for the main assembly facility at Eemshaven, named as the building yard of B G Ireland. Here, these are added to hulls constructed by third-parties, notably by Daewoo Mangalia, in Romania, in line with an arrangement which has already collectively produced some 30 vessels. B G Ireland is of single-deck, double-skin construction with three cargo holds. Access to No 1 is through an extended forecastle which has the fore-part covered by a whaleback structure affording cover to the mooring area, and joined at its aft end with a breakwater erected to provide protection to deck-stowed containers. Each hold is fitted with cell guides, which, in
No 2 hold, can be moved to suit 40ft and 45ft containers as required. Carriage of 20ft/30ft/40ft/45ft, and 49ft units is possible, and 100 reefer plugs are fitted. The hatch covers are of the hydraulic folding type designed, along with the adjacent deck, for maximum permissable stack loads of 60/70tonnes for 20ft units, 80tonnes for 30ft units, and 90tonnes for other sizes. In the holds, the loadings are 70/105tonnes for TEU/FEU. The side and bottom tanks in the double-hull space are separate, and used for fuel or water ballast. A centreline duct keel divides the bottom tanks, and fore and aft passageways are formed at the top of the side tanks, port and starboard. The five-tier superstructure is built on the poop deck and has the wheelhouse on top. In common with many similar designs, it is positioned right aft above the single
CP propeller and rudder, with the engineroom forward of this layout. The upper deck above this space is available for stowage of two FEU stacks. The hull is constructed with both longitudinal and transverse framing, with extra stiffening added in way of the forecastle to counter the effects of the vessel's high speed. Additional stiffening is also introduced in the double bottom area and this allows a tank top loading of 6.37tonnes/m2 to be applied. All the bulkheads are of flush construction and the transverse bulkheads run from shell to shell. The main propulsion plant features a MaK 8M43 medium-speed diesel engine developing 7200kW at 500rev/min. This is connected by way of a flexible coupling to a Flender Navilus reduction gearbox fitted with a PTO to a Stamford 1150kW alternator, arranged for parallel take-over, which supplies the sea load. Two
Scania-powered Stamford 350kW diesel-driven main alternators suitable for parallel running, are also provided. A service speed of 18knots is achieved at 95% MCR from the four-bladed, moderately skewed, 4600mm diameter propeller. For improved manoeuvrability, a 650kW tunnel-type thruster with CP propeller is fitted forward, and the rudder is of the hinged flap type. A thermal oil system is installed to supply heat to the bunker and service tanks. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

FOR this vessel, it has been the intention to build an ecologically safe and economical vessel to the high standards of Marittima Emiliana, applying a lengthy list of RINA class notations. Lines have been optimised by extensive CFD calculations and model tests for low consumption, good seakeeping, minmum vibration, and efficient manoeuvrability. The structure is optimised by FEM. Traceability of records is ensured by relevant class notations for hull and machinery. Fully integrated automation systems for cargo handling, propulsion and engineroom services, are centralised in the engine control room, cargo control room, and wheelhouse. A unique feature of Bianca Amoretti is that the cargo pipe tunnel gives the advantage of a cargo pump room but avoiding the disadvantage of cargo pipes in the cargo tanks, thus combining the characteristics of a standard oil tanker with that of a chemical tanker
The double-skin hull is divided into seven pairs of cargo tanks and two slop tanks by vertically corrugated bulkheads suitable for carrying IMO Type II and III products with densities of 1.025tonnes/m3 and up to 1.54tonnes/m3 partially filled for temperatures up to 80°C. Special care has been taken to ensure superior tankwashing. The double hull extends through the engineroom in way of fuel and lubricating oil tanks. In the cargo pumproom, four Bornemann 600m3/h cargo pumps, driven through bulkhead connections, enable discharge rates of 2400m3/h without vapour return, or 1200m3/h when that facility is adopted. The cargo handling system has been optimised for minimum cargo retention, minimum emissions and shortest turnround times, and is arranged for simultaneous discharge amidships of four segregations with two vapour returns, or for three segregations over the stern with one vapour return. Remote level, temperature, and pressure control of each cargo tanks is carried out by
Saab equipment directly feeding into the automation system. All Rizzio valves in the pipe tunnel are remote controlled. Fresh and dry air ventilation lines, with valves and connections for portable hoses in way of each ventilation hatch, are arranged on deck, with 2 x 15,000Nm3/h and 2 x 5000Nm3/h anti-spark centrifugal fans fitted in deckhouses above cargo area, together with two 5000m3/h dehumidifiers. The tank washing system is sized to suit the simultaneous use of six machines, and can operate with either hot and cold fresh water, or sea water. Each tank is equipped with two Scanjet washing machines, and COW facilities are also provided. A 3250m3/h Smit inert-gas plant is fitted in the engineroom. Mooring arrangements with 16.5tonne-pull winches have been based on OCIMF and Exxon criteria, and optimised for 12 mooring lines ashore. Emergency towage facilities are arranged forward and aft, and a strong point for 2000KN is provided forward. Bianca Amoretti is fitted with an MaK 9M43 main engine having a MCR of 8400kW. Drive to the CP propeller is by way of a Renk vertically offset gearbox. Also connected is a shaft generator which doubles as an auxiliary motor for emergency propulsion, according
RINA class notations for AVM-APS. To ensure enhanced manoeuvrability, both bow and stern thrusters, and a semi-compensated type rudder are fitted. For future installation of an SCR emissions converter, space is available in the funnel. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

BITFLOWER has been designed to serve her owner's trade, which is primarily involved with the transport of molten bitumen, hot pitch, and related products having transportation temperatures up to 250oC. Although complying with the requirements for worldwide operation, the vessel has been particularly equipped in accordance with Finnish and Swedish ice navigation rules, for service along the coasts of
Northern Europe and the Baltic Sea. A notable feature of her design is the arrangement of the bulbous bow, which in both loaded and ballast condition provides optimal facilities for icebreaking, and, additionally, for 'notch travelling' with an icebreaker. The double-skin hull surrounds two cargo holds, into each of which a cargo tank block is installed. These are constructed of semi-rigid steel and placed on composite supports on the tanktop which allow free movement under the influence of thermal expansion, and reduce heat transmission from the tanks, to the double-bottom structure. Similarly, the entire piping system, including the pumps, is flexibly mounted so that expansion can be compensated for without the need for bellows joints. All outside surfaces of the blocks are insulated with a two-layer system of mineral wool, partly sheathed with steel. Cargo is handled from a central pumproom positioned between the holds, using two Bornemann hydraulically operated pumps having a maximum capacity of 500m3/h. These can draw from each tank and deliver through the cargo system to the two deck cross-over lines. Filling lines link in with this system, and interconnections between suction and drop lines allow circulation and cargo trimming between tanks. Two grades can be loaded or discharged from the tanks simultaneously, operating in a predetermined sequence designed to minimise the effect on trim and list. Loading/unloading systems are maintained remotely from a cargo control room, with Saab monitoring equipment measuring cargo, ballast and bunker tank temperatures, pressures and levels, and linked to a loading computer indicating stress and stability information. Cargo heating is accomplished using two 1150kW boilers located in the engineroom and burning heavy fuel. These raise, to 290oC, the temperature of the diathermic oil medium used to maintain the viscosity of the cargo at required levels. A secondary system, heated to 600kW by an exhaust-gas boiler, provides fluid at 180oC to serve bunker tanks, and supply auxiliary equipment and domestic users. Bitflower features a Wärtsilä-Propac machinery installation incorporating a 6L38B main engine, a reduction gearbox with Vulkan flexible couplings, and a
CP propeller. Engine output is 4350kW at 600rev/min, with speed reduced by the gearbox to 155rev/min at the propeller. Electrical power is supplied from a 700kW PTO connected to a Stamford alternator, and the same manufacturer supplied three 700kW diesel- driven sets having Volvo prime movers. The shaft alternator is designed to serve as an auxiliary propulsion source, with a 'power-take-in' (PTI) enabling the alternator to drive the propeller through the gearbox. The wheelhouse serves as a multi-functional control centre and has a layout designed in accordance with
Bureau Veritas notation CNC-1. The large windows give 360deg visability, and equipment includes a complete 'navcom' package supplied by STN ATLAS featuring a NACOS 55-4 integrated navigation and command system, two radars, and two Debeg DGPS navigators. Areas of the space are also designated for cargo control purposes and for use as a conference/reception section. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

SINCE making the transition into shipbuilding, from being one of the world's leading shiprepairers, Hyundai Mipo has quickly earned a high ranking amongst Korea's most successful yards, starting the year under review with an order book of more than 70 ships. Many of these were for a particular speciality of the company: product tankers in the 35,000dwt/37,000dwt and 46,000dwt range, and it is to that latter group of newbuildings that Boraq belongs. The design has been constructed with scantlings suitable for the carriage of cargoes of specific gravity 1.025, and features a single, cambered upper deck without a forecastle, and a double hull enclosing a cargo space divided by a centreline, and transverse bulkheads into 10 pairs of tanks. Two slop tanks are also provided, and the arrangement is suitable for the carriage of IMO
Type II chemicals and petroleum products, including methanol, MTBE and caustic soda. The divisional bulkheads are troughed, and built up on stools, and with all other stiffening fitted externally, the tank surfaces are plain, and coated with inorganic zinc silicate, affording high-grade protection against the often hazardous cargoes carried, and also facilitating easy cleaning. The side tanks, within the double hull, are divided to provide five pairs of tanks, joined with the centrally divided double bottom to form 'L-shaped' water ballast tanks. Piping within the tanks is fabricated from SUS316L stainless steel, and grade AISI 316 material has been used in the manufacture of the 22 Frank Mohn (Framo), hydraulically driven submerged pumps, fitted in each cargo and slop tank. This Norwegian company was involved with the first product tankers built at
Mipo, even modifying its design to suit the ship specification, and has since gone on to supply more than 40 tankers building at the yard. Hydraulic operation of the pumps allows stepless capacity control, enabling optimal handling of all types of chemicals and oil products. An inbuilt stripping system is also a feature. The five-tiers-high, plus wheelhouse, accommodation block is positioned aft above the engineroom, and is linked to the fore end of the vessel by means of a gangway over the fore deck. The funnel and casings are separated from the living quarters provided for a complement of 26, plus six Suez running crew, in order to reduce vibration and noise. They also support the access to the free-fall lifeboat which operates over the stern. A popular choice of main engine for this class of tanker is the MAN B&W 6S50MC-C unit, and Boraq follows that trend by utilising an example of this design supplied from the Hyundai Heavy Industries works. It develops 9480kW at 127rev/min MCR and is directly coupled to a FP propeller. When running at 90% full power, the arrangement produces a service speed of 15.25knots. Three diesel-alternator sets supply electrical services, and the Wärtsilä/Leroy Somer combinations adopted each deliver 900kW. Steam is produced in an
Aalborg 18,000kg/h oil fired boiler, and in a composite boiler of 2000/1300kg/h. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

AFTER a period of financial uncertainty, Szczecin
Shipyard has now re-entered the European shipbuilding market place under a revised, but still identifiable name, and is currently delivering a new class of chemical tanker, understood to be the largest yet available in the world. Bow Sun is the lead vessel of this B588-III series, which has structure confirmed by
DNV to have a 40-year fatigue life. The double hull surrounds a cargo space divided by two longitudinal, and 17 transverse bulkheads, into 18 centre tanks, and eight pairs of wing tanks, all built from solid duplex stainless steel, with the same material used for six cylindrical tanks carried on the upper deck to provide flexibility of cargoes. Transverse bulkheads are horizontally corrugated, with the longitudinal divisions separating centre and wing tanks flush, and of the sandwich type, forming void cofferdams to reduce heat transfer. All stiffening is arranged outside the tanks, providing a smooth surface for cleaning. Side and bottom double-hull spaces form water ballast tanks, except for the aftermost side pair, used for tank cleaning fresh water (TCFW). Bow Sun will be operated in the Seachem Pool by her owner, and is designed to carry IMO Type I, II and III cargoes, petroleum products, vegetable, animal and fish oils, and molasses. Loading/discharging is possible through the midship manifolds at a maximum rate of 3000m3/h with each tank fitted with its own piping system and submerged cargo pump. All piping, valves and pumps are of stainless steel, as are the two sets of heating coils fitted in each tank which circulate hot water heated by steam in two heat exchangers. The latter are installed in a deckhouse forward of the main superstructure. In addition, four of the cargo tanks are fitted with a thermal oil heating system. Tank cleaning uses two 200m3/h pumps, arranged for simultaneous operation with sea and fresh water, each medium having its own heater. The inert gas arrangements feature a Unitor package with nitrogen of 95% purity produced from four 360kW compressors, and generators of 3750m3/h capacity, using hollow- fibre air separation techniques. Tank levels, temperatures and pressures, in cargo, ballast and engineroom tanks, are measured by a Saab TankRadar installation, with that company also supplying high level alarms and calculators. A high-pressure hydraulic system, supplied from five 375kW power packs, operates on a ring main with branches to cargo/ballast tank-cleaning pumps, manifold cranes, bow thruster and mooring equipment. Cargo and ballast valves, however, are driven from an independent hydraulic system. The main engine is a Cegielski-Sulzer 6RTA58TB type, developing 12,750kW at 105rev/min. A CP propeller is fitted, and an 1847kVA alternator operates off the shaft. Three diesel-driven alternators each produce 1782kVA, and steam requirements are served from two auxiliary boilers and an exhaust-gas economiser. Main and auxiliary engines, and the boilers, are arranged to burn RMH55 heavy oil, and class requirements for an unattended engineroom are covered by a Pleiger integrated control and monitoring system (ICMS), which also handles cargo and ballast pumps and valves, and tank cleaning pumps, from the engine and cargo control rooms. A Schilling Monovec rudder provides good dynamic stability, turning, and yaw-control facilities, with manoeuvrability assisted by a 1000kW hydraulically bow thruster. In accordance with MSC Resolution MSCC 35/63, emergency towing arrangements are fitted forward and aft. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

SWELLING the ranks of VLCC tonnage built by
Hyundai during this yard's 30 years of existence is
Capricorn Star, its deadweight of 317,000tonnes registering an almost 22% increase over that of the first vessels to be delivered from the Ulsan shipyard: significantly, two VLCCs for Livanos interests. Increased capacity is not the only point of comparison between the two designs, for Capricorn Star clearly demonstrates the attention now required of shipbuilders (and which was, in the main, unheard of 30years ago), in order to produce an environment-friendly vessel, particularly with regard to tanker tonnage. Typically, for this 21st century design, this calls for a double-skin hull, now extended beyond the cargo spaces to encompass bunker tanks which, in line with current considerations, also include separate low-sulphur tanks. Perhaps less radical, but equally important inclusions, are the IMO-approved incinerator, and the use of a refrigerant in the domestic storeroom installation, which is non-ozone depleting. Capricorn Star is a single-decked vessel without forecastle. The double-hull configuration adopted by
Hyundai is 3.5m wide at the sides and 3.00m deep in the double bottom, with the junction between the side bulkhead and tanktop forming a sloping bilge. The space thus enclosed is combined as port and starboard 'L- shaped' water ballast tanks divided by the centre girder. The cargo space is separated by two longitudinal bulkheads into port, starboard and centre tanks, then subdivided transversely to provide 15 tanks, which, with two slop tanks, are part of an installation designed to handle three grades of cargo simultaneously. Three 5500m3/h steam turbine-powered pumps are installed in a room positioned forward of the machinery space. Cargo and ballast control and monitoring systems are computerised and cover operation of pumps, valves, ullage measurement, and inert gas facilities. Radar beam- type level gauges are fitted to the cargo tanks, and an electro-pneumatic method of sounding is used in the ballast tanks. Crude oil washing facilities are incorporated utilising a 3000m3/h pump, also housed in the pumproom. A six-tier deckhouse is arranged above the engineroom aft, built with the casing and funnel separated from the main structure to minimise vibration and noise transmission. A total of 36 crew members are accommodated in single cabins, with an additional room available for a Suez/repair crew. At the top of the deckhouse is the navigating bridge, arranged with
Hyundai-Kongsberg bridge control also integrated automatic control and monitoring systems, and a Kelvin
Hughes integrated navigation system, all suitable for 'one- man-bridge' operation. Features provided include route planning, manoeuvring for collision, grounding avoidance, and navigation monitoring. The machinery installation includes a Hyundai-MAN
B&W 7S80C main engine, developing 34,650bhp MCR at 79rev/min, and enabling a service speed of 14.60knots to be achieved at the design draught, with 90% full power and allowing a 15% sea margin. A 1500kW alternator driven from the main shaft is one source of electrical power, with three Wärtsilä/Taiyo diesel-driven sets of the same output also available. Two oil-burning boilers each produce 50,000kg/h to satisfy steam demands. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

BUILT to what is, arguably, the most novel ship design to be included in this edition of Significant
Ships, Carmel Ecofresh and a sister are intended for operation on the regular Agrexco (Agricultural Export
Co) service between Haifa, Marseilles and Barcelona, offering some 460,000ft3 of refrigerated space (mainly fruit, vegetables and flowers) on the westbound voyage, and capacity for nearly 700 cars and 900 containerised general cargo slots on the backhaul run. An interesting feature of a uniquely innovative arrangement (originally developed in 1997 by the Danish consultant
International Maritime Advisers for a project planned by the Dutch operator Seatrade) is the adoption of a large refrigerated cargo hold section amidships, on top of which, and separated from it by a horizontal cofferdam, is the accommodation block. Five conventional cellular holds, capable of loading 266TEU including 9ft 6in high boxes, are located, three forward and two aft of this space. Lashing bridges are used on deck where 630TEU can be carried, and electrical connections are arranged for 100 refrigerated containers, with system provision for another 160. Flush pontoon-type lift-on/lift-off covers are fitted to the cargo hatches, built without coamings, which serve the container holds. The hull is double skinned, with side spaces surrounding the container holds, allocated for water ballast, and used for cargo cooling ducts in way of the fully insulated refrigerated hold, where six cargo decks constructed of lightweight sandwich panels are fitted, and subdivision forms a total of 18 compartments. No vertical access is provided to these spaces: loading of palletised cargo and vehicles being accomplished by means of a Macor Neptun side-loader with a two- section, vertically sliding door giving access to deck No 3 starboard side. This installation is arranged to operate at a quay height of approximately 10m above base line, and is fitted with a buffer at the lowest position. Cargo can be handled at a rate of roughly 275 pallets/h or 500cars in 8h, with onward movement to handling areas at each deck, for internal distribution to the cargo holds, carried out using a twin Hortal conveyor-lift system. The cargo refrigeration system is designed to operate in conditions varying from seawater temperatures between 0oC and 32oC, ambient temperatures of -20oC to +35oC, and an ambient relative air humidity of 70%. Hold temperatures can be maintained between -30oC and +13oC, using a GEA-Grenco plant with three chiller units working on the indirect system. R407C is the primary refrigerant, and calcium chloride brine serves as the secondary heat transfer fluid. Cooling of the cargo spaces is effected by a transverse flow, grating-less, recirculating air system. A mechanical ventilation system provides the refrigerated hold with four air changes/hour when carrying reefer cargo, and 10/hour when loaded with cars. Six changes/hour are available to the container holds. Electrical power for the refrigerating plant and ship's services comes from two 1210kVA and two 1820kVA diesel-alternator sets. The machinery installation is positioned right aft, and is of particular note for its use of what is understood to be the first production model Sulzer RT-flex60C main engine to be designed from the outset without a mechanical camshaft and individual fuel pumps. Adoption of the RT-flex concept introduces full electronic control for common-rail fuel injection and exhaust-valve actuation, with benefits in through-life efficiency and environmental performance, through flexibility in matching engine settings to specific criteria at any stage in the voyage cycle. This allows steady running at very slow speeds, with smoke-free operation throughout the load range. The particular unit fitted to Carmel Ecofresh is a 7RT-Flex60C delivering 16,520kW at 114rev/min, directly coupled to a FP propeller for a service speed of 21knots. Manoeuvrability is enhanced by a Brunvoll 1000kW bow thruster and a Becker flap-type rudder, and an anti- heeling system operates during cargo handling. The four-tier accommodation block is topped by a wheelhouse with the conning positions offset to starboard. The third level is given over to ancillary compartments, with crew and officers occupying the two lower levels where single berth en-suite cabins are arranged. An unusual feature is the use of the central area of these decks as an atrium, lit by an overhead skylight and used for recreation purposes and a 'coffee corner'. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

CELANOVA has been built to suit the charter requirements of Repsol YPF, for service around the southern Spanish coasts and Balearic Islands. She is a Type 2G liquefied gas tanker designed to transport semi-refrigerated cargoes (-48oC, 6.5bar, 0.6m3) in five IMO type C independent, stainless steel cylindrical tanks, carried on supports and surrounded by void spaces, within a hull conventionally laid out with machinery and accommodation spaces aft. A Tractebel Gas Engineering (TGE) handling and re-liquefaction plant is installed to deal with gases including propene, propane, commercial propane, propane/butane, anhydrous ammonia, vinyl chloride monomer, i-butane, n-butane, butane, and butadiene. A direct-working system is employed, where re- liquefaction of vaporised cargo is effected by means of three systems - a pressure decrease in the tanks, compression with condensation against seawater, and expansion back into the tanks. Three Sulzer direct-drive, labyrinth piston-type cargo compressors, each with two cylinders for one or two-stage compression, are provided, together with suction separators, flash drum-type cargo economisers, and LPG condensers (three of each). These, and the cargo heater/evaporator, and fresh water heating/cooling system, are fitted with titanium plate heat exchangers, and are installed in a deckhouse abaft the forecastle. Cargo is handled using a 150m3/h electrically driven deepwell pump in each tank, ensuring loading and unloading times, with vapour return, of around 10hours. Two grades of cargo can be transported at the same time, and each can be cooled simultaneously. Two 150m3/h booster pumps are also fitted, arranged to work in series with either one or two cargo pumps as required. A hydraulically operated ESD quick-closing system, using actuators fitted to valves in the cargo and vapour lines at crossovers and tank domes, operates in accordance with the IGC code to provide an emergency stop system, whilst other safety features include a detection system for identifying gas concentrations in hazardous areas of the plant, in accordance with IMO rules, and an inert gas installation, fitted with two absorbers filled with carbon monoxide sieves in the nitrogen production equipment. The machinery installation forms part of a package supplied by IZAR Manises, which includes the MAN
B&W 8S35MC main engine, shafting, FP propeller, and fuel conditioning modules for main and auxiliary engines. The engine develops 5920kW at 173rev/min and is intended to burn fuel of 380cSt at 50oC. Electrical supply is derived from three 900kVA/1000rev/min diesel-alternator sets, and both main and auxiliary engines are served from a central cooling system. A Brunvoll 450kW bow thruster is fitted, and the rudder is of the underhung type. Four decks in the superstructure accommodate the 17-person crew, with each having a single-berth cabin including private toilet facilities. Situated above this arrangement, the wheelhouse is equipped with a console incorporating navigation, control and alarm systems. Cargo loading is monitored to ensure arrangements are within strength and stability safety limits. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

THIS new Fortune Mk II class is one of five bulk carrier designs currently building with various
Chinese yards, all developed by Campbell, and its
Bahamian associate, Algoship Designers. Each class offers individual characteristics aimed at satisfying the differing market requirements of a wide range of operators, and illustrated here by Cinnamon, also later in this review by IVS Viscount, another example of the quintet. Together, the designs complement an earlier entry into the Campbell standard bulk carrier portfolio, the Fantasy, featured in Significant Ships of 1996 with
Clipper Fantasy. The Fortune Mk II class is a worldwide, deepsea transport, Great Lakes-fitted, wide-hatch, multi- purpose, dry cargo, self-trimming bulk carrier, particularly suitable also, for the carriage of parcel size cargoes, steel products and coils, and sawn timber. The latest requirements for handling ballast in restricted waters, and shipboard manning practices have been incorporated, as well as regulations governing pollution, safety and communications. Ice Class 1C classification rules are also complied with. The vessel is divided into six cargo holds by corrugated transverse bulkheads, and laid out with holds 1 and 4 double-skinned. The tanktops are strengthened for heavy cargoes, and the design is suited for multi-port and block loading with two holds slack, and for sailing in 'any hold empty HC-EA' conditions. Even with a beam restriction of 23.70m to suit St
Lawrence requirements, it has been possible to include extra-wide hatch openings to facilitate loading steel pipes and products. Dangerous goods can be loaded in packaged form, and sawn timber carried on deck and hatch covers. Covers are of the end-folding, box-type, designed by
MacGregor and fitted with mechanical ventilation. They are remotely controlled hydraulically by one person and supplied from a separate power system. Also electro-hydraulically operated are three
MacGregor-Hägglund 40-tonne, wire-luffing deck cranes, arranged for grab operation. A feature of the cargo handling arrangements is the provision of an easily operated, hold washing system, coupled to tank storage facilities, to prevent the discharge of dirty water in the St Lawrence Seaway. A raised forecastle and 'B' type freeboard ensure drier decks in loaded conditions, whilst self-trimming grain loading, and strengthening against slamming at light ballast draught (thereby reducing the need to ballast No 4 hold), are salient features of a design aimed at incurring as few restrictions as possible in international trading. With operations in the St Lawrence Seaway given special consideration in this Fortune Mk II series, a bow thruster assists navigation in restricted waters and serves to minimise forward shell damage. Provision for fitting piping, valves and cables in the duct keel and side pipe tunnels, allows easier maintenance, and gives protection against corrosion and weather conditions, for which purpose ballast tanks are heated, and provided with an air-bubbling anti-freeze system. The main engine is a MAN B&W 7S42MC Mk6 unit, directly coupled to an FP propeller, designed to provide sufficient reserve power under normal sea going conditions, and to allow for future fouling. The three
Taiyo alternator sets are driven by Yanmar diesel engines, in an installation designed to provide adequate redundancy. Ballasting is controlled from a central station, and automation and monitoring meets UMS and EO notations. Central fresh water cooling, high- spec coatings, sacrificial anodes in ballast tanks and cathodic protection for the hull, gravity discharge for wing tanks and direct discharge/filling of the ballast hold are particular features. THIS 5884dwt IMO II oil/chemical tanker was built in the Celik Tekne Shipyard for Mediterranea di
Navigazione, Italy, and chartered to API. Cosmo will operate under a five-year time-charter contract renewable for a further four years with API. The vessel is fitted with an integral double hull, an auxiliary propulsion system, and the structure was calculated using the Bureau Veritas Veristar finite- element analytical system, including fatigue checks. This permitted attainment of the class symbol 'Clean Sea -
Clean Air', AVM-APS-NS, Star Hull. Cosmo also boasts the Green Star notation issued by Italian classification society Registro Italiano Navale, which ensures the vessel is environment-friendly. The 12 cargo tanks (10 cargo plus two slop), are divided by vertically corrugated bulkheads, while the bulkheads at the end of the cargo area are flat. Each tank is coated with a phenolic epoxy coating - the Sigma
Phenguard System. The structure of the cargo tanks is designed for loads having a density of up to 1.54tonnes/m³. In addition, between the port and starboard cargo tanks, an emergency valve connection system is fitted. Cargo heating is by means of steam and the plant can raise the temperature of a full cargo from 44ºC to 66ºC in 96 hours. Cargo handling is by means of 12 Frank
Mohn (Framo) submerged, hydraulically operated pumps (each of 200m³/h). For tank cleaning operations, Scanjet fixed and programmable machines are installed (two in each cargo tank and four in each slop space), together with one Framo tank-cleaning pump (120m³/h), and an associated Bloksma tank cleaning heater. A Saab parabolic antenna-type level-gauging system (TankRadar) is provided for each cargo and slop tank. Level, temperature, and inert-gas pressure gauges are fitted, together with high level and overflow alarms, while cargo operations are masterminded from a control room in the accommodation block. Each of the cargo and slop tanks is also fitted with a high velocity pressure/vacuum valve, and two vapour return lines are also provided. The main engine is from the Augsburg works of
MAN B&W; it develops 3360kW at 750rev/min. The
CP propeller is from MAN B&W Alpha Diesel, and three diesel-generator sets (with Yanmar engines) each provide 500kW. A 100kW emergency generator from
Demp is also installed. Like many chemical tankers, Cosmo is fitted with an 800kW A Van Kaick shaft alternator that is able to work, through the reduction gear, as an auxiliary propulsion system. When this system is operating, the maximum speed is 9knots. A Becker high-performance flap rudder and Brunvoll 400kW hydraulically operated bow thruster enhance manoeuvrability. Cosmo also has an integrated navigation system, and the design of the wheelhouse console fulfils all the requirements of OMBO class notation. Emergency towing facilities are arranged forward. Firefighting systems are available, including CO2 for use in the engineroom, and foam, utilising six monitors, on deck. For the high and low-pressure steam pipes that are placed on the open deck, a special pre-insulating system is used in order to prevent heat loss in these lines. Accommodation is provided for 15 permanent crew. A free-fall lifeboat operates over the stern, and a rescue boat and liferafts are also carried. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

SOME 15 cruise ships, with an estimated total value of more than US$9billion, have been delivered by
Fincantieri shipyards over the past decade, into fleets controlled by the Carnival Group, and Costa Fortuna enters service with its Italian-based subsidiary Costa
Crociere, not only as her owner's flagship, but as the largest cruise ship ever to sail under the Italian flag. Built at the Sestri Ponente shipyard (as its first large passenger ship since Michelangelo of 1965), Costa Fortuna has a gross tonnage of 105,000gt and can carry a maximum 3470 passengers in 1358 cabins, of which 63% of the total (853) have an ocean view and 522 have balconies. All cabins are arranged with living and sleeping areas, with the latter normally fitted with twin beds, and with the additional facility in most, of pullman beds or convertible sofas for family occupancy. The living areas are outfitted for maximum comfort with television, radio and telephone. Disabled travellers are catered for with a choice of 26 specially equipped cabins. Central to the public spaces is a nine-deck high atrium surmounted by a glass skylight. This forms the main entrance, and gives access to other decks by means of four panoramic glass lifts. A lobby bar and foyer, and reception counters are located here. The principal entertainment feature is the 1600-seat, three-deck high, show lounge. This provides full bar service, and offers artists a 160m2 stage with revolving platform and orchestra pit. Dining rooms are located forward and aft, both of which extend over two decks and seat 750 and 1050 diners, respectively. Other facilities include a grand bar with dance floor, casino and sports bar; also a disco occupying two decks and equipped with a glass dance floor and screen wall. Again spread over two decks is the lido buffet with glass skylight and six self-service counters. Children are catered for with a dedicated playroom and open space with pool, and fitness requirements are served from sauna, massage and treatment rooms. A sliding, telescopic roof covers the aft swimming pool, lido area with bar, band stage, snack counters, and jacuzzi. The internal decoration and choice of materials used is aimed principally at the European and Mediterranean market, and includes extensive use of marble, stone, fibre optics, automated lighting, and art works. It follows a general design produced to limit noise generation and transmission. Noise levels at 95% contractual propulsion motor output under normal operating conditions will not exceed 50dB(A) in passenger suites and de-luxe cabins; 55dB(A) in standard cabins, and 60dB(A) in public spaces. Noise reduction between cabins is not to be less than 40dB. Although there have been moves by some owners and builders in recent years to introduce 'combination power systems' such as COGES (Combined Gas and
Steam Turbine, Integrated Electric Drive System), and
CODAG (Combined Diesel Engine and Gas Turbine), Carnival/Costa have opted for a straight diesel-electric propulsion plant for Costa Fortuna and sister Costa
Magica. The installation is based upon six ABB alternators: four driven by Sulzer 16ZAV40S diesel engines, and two arranged with 12ZAV40S prime movers. These supply power to the main network to satisfy ship's services and auxiliary machinery, and to two ABB main propulsion motors having an output of 2 x 20MW. Unlike Costa's earlier Costa Atlantica (Significant Ships of 2000), where two Azipod propulsion units were used to drive the ship, twin conventional CP propellers have been adopted for these new vessels, each supplied via a stepless AC/AC thyristor cyclo-converter. The contractual service speed to be achieved from this arrangement is 22.50knots with the propulsion motors producing 17MW. A total of six Kamewa thrusters are installed: fitted with CP propellers, these operate three forward and three aft. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

IN Significant Ships of 2002, reference was made to the inevitable similarity between the many vehicle carrier designs being offered by shipbuilders throughout the world, resulting from parameters, introduced by operators, covering capacity, on board movement of vehicles, also compatibility with loading/discharging berths, within which designers must work, and from which the 'floating garage' concept has evolved. Courageous Ace displays little obvious variation from what is now the unmistakeable, and often much maligned, characteristics of these vessels; however, there are differences from the norm which Minami
Nippon considers to be sufficiently important as to merit patent applications. The differences have been introduced into a design, which has been developed jointly with Universal Shipbuilding Corporation, a company formed last year following a merger between
Hitachi and NKK under Japan's shipbuilding rationalisation project. In particular, the problem of 'leeway', where the effect of wind on the high sides pushes the vessel sideways, has been addressed, whilst the bow has been aerodynamically rounded and 'bevelled' above the bow- line, to reduce pressure from head winds. In addition, wind channels have been introduced along the sides of the vessel at the top of the garage deck (the additional cargo space built above the upper deck) and these help to maintain a straight course. Test results have shown that an approximately 20% reduction in aerodynamic pressure can be achieved at the bow in winds of 15m/sec, resulting in a 4% improvement in fuel efficiency, with a further 6% benefit anticipated from the minimising of leeway. Together these measures can yield a speed increase of 1 knot, depending on wind direction. These improvements in fuel efficiency also contribute to overall energy conservation by reducing unwelcome
CO2 , NOx and SOx emissions, from a machinery installation centred on a Mitsubishi 8UEC60LS main engine built by Kobe Diesel. This develops 14,160kW at 100rev/min MCR, to drive the vessel at 20knots when operating at 85% full power (12,036kW) and 94.7rev/min, and allowing a 15% sea margin. A further device incorporated in the vessel's design by Minami
Nippon is a propeller boss cap fin (PBCF), the simple but effective device from Mitsui OSK Techno Trade, which is fitted to the FP propeller, and claimed to enhance its efficiency by 4%-5%. Electrical power is derived from three Daihatsu/Nishishiba 1000kW diesel- alternator sets, and steam requirements are obtained from a 1500kg/h water-tube boiler. A Kawasaki 1250kW thruster is installed at the bow to further assist manoeuvrability. As noted above, the basic design of Courageous Ace conforms to the now accepted standard layout for this type of vessel, with 10 fixed and two portable vehicle decks within the main hull, and additional space available in the 'garage' which almost entirely covers the upper deck. Shore access is over a starboard quarter door/ramp, and through port and starboard side doors, on to deck 7 (freeboard deck), with fixed internal ramps moving vehicles between decks. Maximum capacity is calculated as 6400 standard-size cars. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

FOR many years, Kawasaki's newbuilding output has included vessels which display the yard's accumulated knowledge of liquid gas transportation technology. A large number of these vessels also incorporate the results of the company's on-going research into marine hydrodynamics, and feature successful energy-saving devices such as the Kawasaki
Rudder Bulb System, typically fitted in conjunction with propeller fins, contra-fins or semi-ducts. Crystal
Marine benefits from this adaptation, but is more especially notable as the first vessel to adopt Kawasaki's latest hull-form development, designated the 'SEA-
Arrow' (an acronym for 'Sharp-Entrance-Angle bow as an Arrow'). LPG tankers are often restricted in length to suit port facilities, and, consequently, are bluff-bowed, so, to compensate, cargo tanks are extended well forward in order to maximise cargo capacity. At higher speeds, however, this characteristic produces high bow waves and increases resistance. With this in mind, Kawasaki has introduced this new bow design, identified by a vertical stem, in an attempt to reduce the problem as applied to LPG tankers. It dispenses with today's normal, bulbous bow, yet retains the beneficial effects of that feature and is claimed to halve bow-resistance drag, allowing the vessel to maintain the same speed as a 'conventional-hull' LPG tanker of similar size, but expending between 5% and 10% less horsepower, alternatively gaining 0.3knots/0.5knots speed increase when operating at the same horsepower. Crystal Marine is a flush-decked vessel, arranged with four holds having topside wing tanks, and hopper side tanks which are combined with centrally divided double-bottom tanks. All are mainly utilised for carrying water ballast. Whereas most LPG carriers of this size are configured with a forward deeptank to supplement the fore peak for ballasting purposes, this design uses the new bow hull form to provide only a larger forepeak tank. Fuel is carried in a cross bunker forward of the engineroom, and in side tanks within the machinery space, which is arranged right aft and is surmounted by an accommodation deckhouse, separated from the engine and funnel casings to reduce any noise and vibration problems, Within the holds, four independent, insulated, Type 2G prismatic cargo tanks are fitted, supported by a load-bearing structure developed from Kawasaki's long experience, and benefitting from an FEM analysis which examined the interaction of movements and forces working between the tanks and adjoining hull members. Crystal Marine is designed to carry various propane/butane loading ratios, and to provide for flexible cargo operations, the tanks are of different capacities, and arranged so that more than two kinds of cargo can be carried at any one time, using a cargo piping system which allows the re-liquefaction plants to be operated independently with respective tanks. Cargo is handled by means of eight Shinko submerged centrifugal pumps, each having a capacity of 600m3/h. In addition, four eductors are fitted, and there is one booster pump of 500m3/h. Four re-liquefaction plants are installed in a deckhouse on the upper deck, with the driving motors located in an adjoining compartment and connected by shafts through the bulkhead. Also included in the system are a cargo heater/vaporiser, an aeration fan and an inert gas generator. Control of the loading/unloading operation, and of the ballasting systems, is carried out from a console in the cargo/ballast control room, arranged with three visual display terminals indicating the positions of valves, and monitoring alarm systems. Crystal Marine is fitted with a MAN B&W 7S60MC-C main engine constructed by Kawasaki Heavy Industries, now operating separately from the shipbuilding arm of the business under Japan's recent shipbuilding rationalisation programme. MCR output is 14,000kW at 105rev/min and NCR is 11,900kW. The engine is directly coupled to a FP propeller working in conjunction with a Kawasaki rudder bulb and fin. Electrical supply is from three Yanmar/Taiyo diesel- alternator sets, each producing 960kW when running at 900rev/min, and bridge control is by means of a
NABCO system. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

THE Izar Group's decision to enter into dredger construction quickly opened up an association with
Belgian specialist Jan de Nul, which was marked in
Significant Ships of 2002 by the 16,500m3 capacity Kaishuu, built in the Sestao yard. Two smaller (4400m3) vessels have now followed from the shipbuilder's Gijon yard, intended principally for maintenance contracts, but equally suitable for construction and beach replenishment work. Their design is laid out with wheelhouse and accommodation well forward and machinery aft. Between these the hopper hold is contained within wide side spaces, and features sloping bottoms directing spoil to a single row of four, hinged, hydraulically operated, box shaped, double doors of the positive opening type. Dredging is by means of a single 900mm suction pipe operating over the starboard side to depths of 21m, or, using an extension piece, 24.3m, working at an angle of about 55deg, and using dragheads fitted with high pressure water jets to loosen compacted soil. Discharge is achieved by dumping through the bottom doors, or by means of a shallow-water dumping valve arranged to operate without protruding below the vessel's base line. Alternatively, a bow connection enables spoil to be pumped ashore through a floating pipeline. Excess water can be emptied prior to dredging using the dredge pump, and remote controlled overflow arrangements are included. A diesel-electric power system is installed providing balanced supply distribution in all working conditions. It uses two Anglo Belgian Corporation (ABC)
DZC12V/2812kW main engines as prime movers for the twin INDAR 2700kW alternators which supply ship's services, dredging, and propulsion/manoeuvring requirements. Unusually for this type of vessel, the latter employs twin Schottel azimuthing units fitted with 2600mm diameter CP propellers. These operate in nozzles under the stern, and are each driven by 2150kW motors. Also fitted in the manoeuvring system is a
Schottel 550kW bow thruster. The dredge pump is a single-wall unit driven through a gearbox by a squirrel cage motor, which, like others in the installation, is fitted with frequency converter speed control. When trailing, the pump operates at about 1250kW, and when pumping ashore the maximum 3000kW is utilised. Other uses for this pump include cargo dilution, and removal of excess water from the hopper. Two 1200kW jet pumps are provided, operating at 7500m3/h when working in tandem carrying out hopper jetting duties, and 1750m3/h in series for draghead jetting. These pumps also supply the degassing installation. Francesco di Giorgio is designed for easy maintenance with many components oversized for longer life, and capable of simple removal for repair. A complete azimuthing unit is amongst the spares carried on board, and it, together with bowthruster and anodes on sea chests can all be replaced whilst the vessel is afloat. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

ACOMMITMENT by the Italian Grimaldi Group to the ro-ro shipping mode has already been highlighted in Significant Ships through the inclusion of ferries Majestic (1993) and La Superba (2002), vessels designed primarily to operate a link between north and south Italy, thus assisting a much desired reduction in the road traffic congestion in those regions. Alongside this operation, the group has expanded its ro-ro involvement into international sectors, evidenced by its successful North Europe/South America, and North
Europe/West Africa Lines, supplementing shorter sea services around European and Mediterranean ports. Much of this trade is generated by the logistic chain of the motor industry, and has been enhanced by the introduction by fixed-day sailings. Whilst dedicated car-carriers are also included in the fleet, vessels such as Grande Nigeria, which can accommodate sizeable intakes of new cars as well as handling containers and ro-ro cargo, have an important role to play in Grimaldi operations, demonstrated by the building of this five-ship class, which follows on almost immediately from another, recently completed, similar quintet. The design presents an unusual profile with a deep hull extending as high as No 8 (upper) deck. Above this is built a four-tier superstructure covering most of the aft-half of the hull, and this is topped by a deckhouse which incorporates the wheelhouse, and accommodation for 33 crew, and perhaps unusually for this type of vessel, 12 passengers. A cargo hold extends between deck 1 (tanktop) and main deck (No 3), with longitudinal bulkheads P&S forming a double hull throughout its length, utilised for ballast and fuel tanks. In all, capacity is provided over 12 fixed and hoistable decks, for approximately 3485 'European' size cars and 1717TEU, with internal stowage for some of the latter available when the hoistable decks 4 and 5 (normally dedicated to cars) are raised. Lane length available for ro-ro cargo is 3590m, and total cargo area available amounts to 42,220m2. A typical voyage 'mix' of these cargo variants would be 2500 cars, 2500 lane metres of ro-ro cargo, and 850TEU. Ro-ro cargo enters at the starboard side of deck No 3, over a MacGregor stern quarter ramp/door approximately 35m long and presenting a minimum/maximum driveway of 8m/15.5m. It is made up of three sections, angled at 39deg to the transverse plane to allow for access whilst the vessel is berthed alongside a quay. The arrangement is designed to accept heavy-lift cargoes up to 250tonnes weight and 6m high x 6m wide. Part of the first ramp-section functions as a watertight door when closed. The internal layout of Grande Nigeria features fixed and hoistable ramps to provide swift cargo movement with minimal damage throughout the vessel, making the design acceptable to shippers, particularly of heavy, rolling equipment. Additional cargo space is available on the open part of the upper deck (No 8), where two 40tonne capacity hydraulic deck cranes suitable for lifting containers and project cargo are installed. The cargo spaces are served by a mechanical ventilation plant and protected by a CO2 fire extinguishing system. The main engine is installed aft, and is a Sulzer 8RTA62U-B unit developing 18,290kW MCR at 115rev/min. A shaft-driven alternator is fitted, producing 1440kW of electric power, backed up by four diesel-driven alternator sets, each of 1080kW output. Steam is supplied from a composite oil-fired/exhaust- gas boiler generating 2.5tonnes/h. A service speed of 19.7knots is achieved from the single CP propeller when operating at about 85% MCR, and 1600kW thrusters are fitted forward and aft. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

HYUNDAI'S output of more than 1000 ships in a lifetime of no more than 30 years, has included ships of all shapes and sizes, equivalent to some 77.5million dwt. Accounting for one million tonnes of that total has been at least 39 gas carriers, of both LNG and LPG types, clearly emphasising the company's experience with that class of vessel. Greek-owned Hellas
Nautilus falls into the category of a 82,000m3 LPG/NH3 carrier which is fully refrigerated. The hull is flush-decked, without forecastle, but has a sunken mooring deck aft. The cargo space is of single- skin construction with top and bottom wing tanks, and is divided into four holds, each of which contains an independent and self-supporting, prismatic cargo tank. These are built from low-temperature carbon steel and insulated with 120mm thick polyurethane foam covered with 0.5mm aluminium-zinc steel sheeting. The double- bottom tanks are separated at the centreline by a pipe tunnel, and form common ballast tanks with the adjacent bottom wing tanks. The piping systems are designed for low temperature (-48oC) Type 2G cargoes including anhydrous ammonia, 1,3 butadiene, butane (ISO and normal), butylenes, commercial propane and propylene, and can handle two grades simultaneously. No internal coatings are applied to the tanks and all piping is constructed from 304L or 316L stainless steel, with the latter material used also for the
Hamworthy KSE Svanehøj vertical deepwell cargo pumps. Two of these are installed in each cargo tank and have a duty of 600m3/h, allowing a discharge time for a full cargo of approximately 18h, excluding stripping. Loading cargo is accomplished in about the same time, based on vapour discharge back to shore. The reliquefaction plant is located in a compressor and pumproom on the main deck, and provides a system which is also capable of handling two cargo grades simultaneously. Hyundai Heavy Industries supplied the 6S60MC-C main engine, and the FP 7000mm diameter propeller, which it drives at an MCR rating of 13,560kW, when running at 105rev/min. The corresponding service speed of 16.75knots is achieved at NCR output and allowing 15% sea margin. Hyundai also provided the three 1200kW alternator sets which satisfy electrical requirements, and steam is generated from a Kangrim oil fired boiler with a performance of 5000kg/h. APROJECTED 50% increase in Spain's consumption of gas has initiated this contract, for vessels equalling in capacity the largest yet built, which re-introduces construction of LNG tankers into the country's shipyards after a lapse of more than 30 years. Although designed to load/unload at all the world's current terminals, Inigo Tapias will normally be employed transporting gas from Trinidad and Tobago to Spain, with hull form, propulsion and power systems, developed for maximum energy economy on what is virtually a liner service. To this end, Schneekluth wake-equalising ducts have been fitted at the stern, in front of a five-bladed FP propeller, in an arrangement claimed to reduce power requirements by 1.50%. The proven reliability of steam turbine propulsion, along with the ability of that system to burn boil-off gases in the boilers, is demonstrated by the fitting of a conventional (for this type of vessel) machinery installation centred upon a Kawasaki package, comprising a main propulsion turbine developing 28,000kW at 83rev/min, two 3150kW turbo-generators, main feed pump turbines, and reversible double- reduction gearing, all assembled at the shipbuilder's
Ferrol works. A 3150kW diesel-alternator set is installed and an 1800kW electric bow thruster is fitted. The two
Mitsubishi boilers each have an output of 65,000kg/h and are arranged to burn heavy oil, boil-off gas, or a combination of both. Inigo Tapias is a single-deck vessel with sunken aft deck, and all accommodation and navigating spaces aft, built with principal dimensions which differ slightly from those of other, similar sized vessels (see Significant
Ships of 2002), resulting in a slightly longer, narrower hull form. This is in order to meet a beam restriction on the building berth at the Sestao yard. The machinery casings and accommodation are not integrated, in order to minimise vibration transmission, and the design satisfies the requirements of a Type 2G tanker suitable for the carriage of liquid natural gas of SG 0.46 at -163oC. Maximum boil off rate is 0.15% cargo volume. The four cargo tanks, which extend above the deck to form a trunk, are built within a complete double-hull structure with integral spaces utilised as water ballast tanks, in accordance with a Gaz Transport/Technigas
NO96-E2 membrane technique, using Invar stainless steel sheet with a 36% nickel content for the primary and secondary barriers. Between these, and the secondary barrier/shell, perlite granules in plywood boxes are inserted. The cargo handling systems can discharge the vessel in 12 hours using two 1700m3/h electric submerged pumps, and one 50m3/h stripping pump, in each tank, supplemented by a 530m3/h emergency cargo pump. Each tank has a liquid manifold; vapour manifold; stripping manifold and cooling manifold, with the liquid pipelines running to the bottom of the tanks and the vapour line connected at the top. Included in the cargo systems are 2 x 30,000m3/h high-duty, and 2 x 8000m3/h low-duty, compressors; two 23,000kg/h main heaters and 1 x 20,000kg/h LNG vaporiser. The forcing vaporiser capacity is 6950kg/h; the inert gas generator is rated at 15,000Nm3/h and the two nitrogen generators each produce 120Nm3/h. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

THE new Valiant class is another of five bulk carrier designs currently building in various Chinese yards, developed by GTR Campbell in conjunction with its
Bahamian associate Algoship Designers, and introduced in this review with the Great Lakes-fitted
Cinnamon. IVS Viscount is larger than that Fortune Mk 2 vessel, and is offered as a high-specification, Handysize bulker, of robust construction for long, reliable service and optimal life cycle cost, which meets anticipated IACS and IMO regulatory requirements. A raised forecastle and Class B freeboard ensure drier decks in loaded conditions, with self-trimming grain loading and strengthening against slamming at light ballast draught (which reduces the need to ballast No 3 hold). These are salient features of a structural design which contains only a minimum quantity (around 16%) of high-tensile steel in low stress areas only, and which complies with ABS Ice Class DO rules. Troughed transverse bulkheads form five cargo holds, arranged within a double hull space which has ballast tanks in the lower parts, and full-length, port and starboard galleries above; these carry pipelines and electric cables, and serve as fore and aft access passageways. Double- bottom ballast, bilge and bunker piping, with associated valves, runs through a duct keel, since no pipes or cables are laid on deck. The structural arrangements in and around the cargo spaces provide a smooth and flush interior and allow wide hatch openings with minimum overhangs; hatches are closed by one-man-operated, hydraulic, end-folding, double-skin steel covers with integral mechanical ventilation. Cargo handling is by means of four 30tonne/26m hydraulic cranes with grab facilities, fitted on the centreline. The holds are suitable for the carriage of a wide range of cargoes, including specified dangerous goods, and are served by explosion-proof exhaust fans and natural ventilation, with provision made for gas and temperature monitoring. For added safety, water ingress alarms cover all holds, galleries, duct keel, and forecastle. Main propulsion is by a Yichang-Sulzer 6RTA48TB slow-speed diesel engine, developing 7650kW at 116.3rev/min and directly coupled to a FP propeller running in a wake-improving nozzle, for a service speed of 14.50knots. Sufficient reserve power is provided for regular economic performance, with cover for fouling and ageing. Three 560kW alternator sets driven by dual-fuel (heavy or diesel oil) diesel engines are fitted, whilst the emergency set is sized to provide safety power to the engineers' workshop, galley, and domestic fresh water system, in addition to satisfying its statutory SOLAS performance. Arrangements for safe, simple and user- friendly operation with the least manpower include centralised ballast control, ergonomically arranged engineroom, an engine control room with automation and monitoring systems, and remote bridge control. Central fresh water cooling is provided, and GRP ballast piping, high-specification ballast tank coatings, and sacrificial anodes should ensure a low-maintenance service life. The wheelhouse offers 270deg vision and was specially designed to ensure a logical layout. A freefall lifeboat, a rescue craft, and inflatable liferafts are carried. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS
