JUMBO Javelin is the first of two sister ships ordered from the Damen Group by Dutch heavy-lift specialist
Jumbo. Constructed mainly by the shipbuilder's
Romanian subsidiary, final fitting out - including the installation of what are understood to be the largest capacity cranes of their type in the world to date - has been carried out in The Netherlands. Naturally, the cranes are the predominant feature of these vessels, however, there are numerous other design innovations to consider, many arising from the accumulated experience of builder, owner, and crane maker. The hull configuration follows that of other ships of this type, with machinery room right aft and superstucture built forward at the break of the forecastle. Between this and the stern, the arrangement provides for a completely obstruction-free deck area extending over a single, open hold, closed by pontoon hatch covers. Similar tweendeck covers are also provided, and these can be positioned in the hold at 15 positions over a height range of 4m. Perhaps the most interesting innovation sees the deckhouse offset to starboard to allow extra-long items of cargo to be carried along the port side. The cranes are mounted on the starboard side of the vessel, with stiffening built into the hull structure, and for additional stability when lifting operations are in progress, two stabilising pontoons are supplied, with provision for the future fitting of a third foreseen. These items are stowed aft on the casing top when not in use. Also of note is the folding breakwater mounted on the portside of the deckhouse, which is turned into a protective position in order to prevent water progressing on to the deck, thus qualifying for a classification notation, allowing hatchcoverless sailing. The two mast-type cranes fitted to Jumbo Javelin are the largest yet constructed by Rotterdam-based Huisman-
Itrec, their 800tonne lifting capacity surpassing the previous largest by some 250tonnes. They are capable of performing this exceptional duty at 28m outreach, and at all slewing angles, and when each is operating over the port side at 22.5deg. Modifications to the design are already perceived which will increase this outreach to 32m, whilst the addition of 'super-fly-jibs' with a maximum lifting capability of 660tonnes is under way. At their original designed rating, when working in tandem, a combined lift of 1600tonnes can be achieved from the cranes, whose main hoist can operate at speeds ranging from 2.3m/min to 7.8m/min. Each crane is also fitted with an auxiliary hoist, rated at 37.5tonnes, which can work at speeds of 25m/min to 40m/min, and has two tugger winches having a maximum pulling capacity of 25tonnes each. Slewing range is up to 210deg in each direction. A heel control system is fitted: developed by Alewijnse Marine, it is integrated with the ship's alarm, monitoring, and bilge/ballast/heeling arrangements, into a new automation system for optimal flexibility. The machinery installation is split between two enginerooms aft, with the main auxiliaries and separator room located between the two shaftlines, angled inboard and behind which a single rudder is fitted. Each train is driven by an MAN B&W Augsburg-built 9L32/40 main engine, which develops 4320kW at 750rev/min before speed reduction in a gearbox to 136.36rev/min at the propeller. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

DESPITE her intended employment on a relatively short, inter-island service - a 42mile voyage linking Madeira and Porto Santo, Lobo Marinho is arranged for unrestricted navigation, and, as a consequence, is remarkably well equipped, offering a full range of facilities for a summer complement of 1157 passengers. Because the service is a daytime operation, only 3 x 2 berth de-luxe cabins are provided on deck 7, for those requiring some privacy on their journey. However, ample seating is arranged throughout the vessel in various lounges, a cafeteria, restaurant, multi-media and games rooms, to accommodate all travellers. In addition, seats and sun- loungers are available on three decks, and 110 aircraft- type reclining seats are provided in a dedicated lounge (deck 6). Another amenity is a two-deck-high, 120-seat cinema, located under the main car deck. The design features a double-skin hull, with the intermediate space utilised as water ballast tanks, heeling tanks and, above the car decks, access and ventilation trunks and workshops. There are a total of eight decks (the tanktop being No 1), and, apart from the cinema and an adjacent multi-media centre, all public spaces are situated above the vehicle space, on decks 5 (upper deck), 6, 7 and 8. Passengers enter through hydraulically operated doors, port and starboard, at deck 5, into a large reception area giving access, forward, to a full- width lounge, and aft, to eating facilities comprising a self-service cafeteria, and a restaurant, together with kitchens. Crew accommodation occupies 44 cabins spread within the forward areas of decks 2, 3, 4, 6 and 7. As with many contemporary ferries, the navigating bridge is not at top deck level: its position well forward on deck 6 being surmounted by a first-class observation lounge on deck 7 and a sun deck on deck 8. Vehicle access is by means of a 12.40m long (including flaps) x 12m wide foldable ramp/door, over the stern on to deck No 3, which is arranged to stow 95, 4.2m x 1.9m private cars. Hoistable car decks are fitted port and starboard between decks 3 and 5, and these allow an additional 50 cars to be carried, with the end panels forming access ramps. Free height above deck 3 is 4.20m, reduced to 2.24m in way of the intermediate deck (No 4). Operation of this deck is by means of hydraulic jigger winches. Alternative vehicle carrying combinations include 14, 7.2m x 2.435m trailers together with 100 cars, and 6TEU with 129 cars. Stowage of this cargo utilises flush securing fittings in the decks. Two Caterpillar MaK 16M32C engines provide propulsion power, with each developing 8000kW at 750rev/min and coupled, via reduction gearboxes, to twin CP propellers carried on extended shafts under the stern. Electrical power is derived from three
Mitsubishi/Volvo Penta/Stamford alternator sets, each rated at 1110kW when running at 1500rev/min. Ancillary equipment includes Rolls-Royce Neptune 1000 retracting fin stabilisers, port and starboard, a Lips 500kW lateral type bow thruster, and twin rudders suspended aft of the propellers. CONSTRUCTION of this vessel is marked by a number of 'firsts'. She is the first delivery from the recently formed Kvaerner Philadelphia shipyard, located within the Philadelphia Naval Base, and the first new ship to be built at the base since the US Navy
Amphibious Command Vessel Blue Ridge, 34 years ago. Manukai and her sister Maunawili are also the first additions to the Matson fleet since 1992, when RJ
Pfeiffer entered service from the NASSCO shipyard (Significant Ships of 1992), and, like that vessel, they are intended for operations on their owner's long- established Los Angeles/Oakland/Seattle to Honolulu route. Re-development of the Philadelphia shipyard has drawn heavily on assistance from Kvaerner's European shipbuilding subsidiaries, particularly the Warnow yard, and the design of this pioneer vessel, which is designated the Philadelphia CV2600 container ship, is based on that German shipyard's own CV2600 series. Particularly noteworthy in this modified Philadelphia version is the inclusion of 'green ship' features such as grey and bilge water holding tanks, green water tanks, and the use of tin-free coatings. No asbestos products or PCBs are included, and the engines are arranged for reduced noxious gas emissions. Building Manukai has served as a valuable training exercise for the new company as a prelude to entering the commercial market place, and has been undertaken in accordance with US Government Jones Act requirements, as part of a Title XI financing programme. The double-hull layout follows the now conventional container ship configuration, with a single deck, forecastle, and sunken aft mooring deck. Eleven hatches fitted with lift-on/lift-off pontoon covers serve the six cargo holds, which are arranged forward and aft of a three-quarter-aft, eight-tier superstructure providing accommodation for 21 crew, three supernumaries and four-man riding crew. The forward hold is accessed through an extended forecastle, and is protected by a breakwater. In common with the other cargo spaces, it is fitted with cell guides suitable for 20ft/40ft containers. Stowage is also arranged on the hatch covers, and over the mooring deck, for units ranging in size from 20ft to 53ft. Reefer plugs are provided for 260 containers on deck and 40 in the hold, served by a Seacos monitoring system. As the series designation would suggest, TEU capacity for this new KPSI product was intended to be around 2600, however after this notation had been agreed, the addition of an extra deckhouse tier allowed higher container stacks to be loaded, increasing capacity to 2900TEU, then, by lengthening No 4 hold and modifying the aft deck to carry larger containers, total
TEU capacity became 3000. Manukai is powered by a Hyundai-built MAN B&W main engine, developing a maximum continuous rating of 28,800kW at 104rev/min, and driving a FP propeller of medium-skew design for a speed of 22.50knots, when running at 90% full power and a sea margin of 20%. Electrical power is derived from four MaK diesel- driven alternator sets, two of which produce 1360kW and two 1530kW. Steam generation is by means of a composite boiler producing 3200kg/h from each side. Manoeuvring is assisted by fitting a Brunvoll 1300kW bow thruster. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

Illustration shows sister Tempera. LONG-TERM endeavours to make the Northern
Sea Route across Russia a commercially viable proposition by opening up the region's valuable oil and gas resources to all-year trading, have been boosted by the entry into service of Mastera, and earlier sister
Tempera, introducing as they do, an innovative method for navigating in ice. Traditionally, all vessels sail forward, with a fore-end hull form shaped to provide the optimum propulsive efficiency and sea keeping properties. For vessels sailing in ice, however, achieving these values is generally contrary to the requirements for efficient icebreaking: conversely, optimising icebreaking features adversely effects normal performance. Tests carried out with existing vessels indicated that icebreaking qualities were improved when running astern, with ice resistance cut by about 50%, but the reality of the idea was not fully realised until the development of podded electrical propulsion allowed the use of a unit which could be turned to drive the vessel either forward or aft. In the latter mode, not only does the unit 'drill' through the ice, but the action of the propeller washes ice off the underwater aft body, and reduces resistance. With the adoption of these patented so-called double-acting principles by Kvaerner
Masa-Yards and the Finnish manufacturer of Azipods, ABB, into a ship, Sumitomo designers could now proceed to develop a hull with a fore body optimised for normal sailing in open water, and aft end lines suitable for running astern in thick ice. For these particular ships, the propeller and fin were also designed by Sumitomo. Ordered from Sumitomo Heavy Industries, but completed by its successor SHI-ME, following the recent re-organisation of the Japanese shipbuilding industry, Mastera is classed by Lloyd's Register with the ice notation 1AS, and, with her sister, is the largest tanker built to this Super ice-class standard. The vessel is laid out with a single deck and no forecastle, and machinery and deckhouse three-quarters aft. The double-hull structure is specially reinforced and has a fatigue life of 40years, and double-skin cofferdams surround the bunker tanks, with a double bottom protecting the pump room. The cargo space is divided by a centreline bulkhead to form six pairs of cargo tanks and two slop tanks, served by three electrically driven pumps, each rated at 3500m3/h, backed up by a 300m3/h stripping pump. Ballast is handled by two pumps of 3000m3/h and 2500m3/h duty, positioned in the pumproom. The propulsion system is centred on a 16,000kW, ABB Azipod propulsion unit fitted aft. This can rotate through 360deg and, in particular, can be turned through 180deg as required, in order to drive the vessel stern-first in ice conditions, or bow-first in open water. A special feature included on Mastera and her sister is a fin below the hub. Power is derived from a diesel-electric system employing five ABB alternators, driven by two Wärtsilä 9L38B, two 6L38B, and one 6L26A, diesel engines, with the sets producing outputs of 2 x 6320kW, 2 x 4220kW and 1 x 1860kW, respectively. This complete diesel-electric podded azimuthing propulsion system was subjected to a failure modes and effects analysis (FMEA) as part of a risk assessment carried out by Lloyd's Register. Port and starboard funnel uptakes are led through the deckhouse and between them, an extension to the normal forward- facing wheelhouse provides a full view aft for navigating whilst the vessel is sailing astern, and is complete with full steering and control equipment. Two Rolls-Royce 1750kW bow thrusters are fitted. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

Photograph of Midnight Sun by Neil Rabinowitz
FEW merchant ship designs can have been subjected to so thorough a technical and economic analysis, as that carried out by owner and builder over a three-year period, prior to construction commencing on Midnight Sun and sister vessel North Star. The purpose of these investigations was to find the most suitable replacement tonnage for the TOTE service, which links Tacoma, Washington State, and Anchorage, Alaska. An ability to operate a voyage occupying 66 hours each way on a weekly round trip schedule, in weather conditions described as equivalent to heavy, North Sea type for half of the year, was an important consideration. Compliance with stringent environmental controls following on from the Exxon
Valdez and other accidents, enforced by IMO, US
Environmental Protection Agency and port authorities, also needed to be incorporated. Selection of propelling machinery fell between straight diesel and various diesel-electric options, with choice finally falling upon a twin-screw, direct-drive, diesel-electric installation with four MAN B&W 9L58/64 main engines, each linked with an Alstom 14,215kVA alternator to supply the two propulsion motors, also of Alstom manufacture, which drive the propellers. Two MAN B&W/Alstom auxiliary alternators are also fitted as part of a system which not only satisfies the vessel's electrical requirements, but, by various combinations of the six machines, can produce service speeds of between 12knots and 25.3knots. Even one propulsion motor and one screw will develop 16knots. Choice of this method of propulsion was also influenced by its lesser requirement for casing space than alternatives, with consequent reduced impact upon on-board trailer access and movement, thereby speeding up cargo handling. Other features of the design include measures to cope with damage to pump and shafting seals (hence sterntube lubricating oil leakages) from the severe silt erosion caused by glacial deposits in Alaskan waters. For this reason, FP, rather than CP propellers have been fitted. Environmental issues considered include reduced noxious fuel emissions, and the provision of a bunker tank forward of the engineroom, protected by a double- hull structure. Ballasting is effected using fresh water, again to reduce silt-damage to structure, piping and pumps, and to eliminate the transfer of non-indigenous species likely with sea water ballast. About 90% of ballast capacity is permanently locked in prescribed tanks, with the remainder able to be transferred as required to suit trim conditions. Midnight Sun is designed to carry its cargo of containers on trailers, with some capacity also available for cars and other road vehicles. Five covered, fixed decks are available, above which there is an uncovered 'spar' deck, protected by the forecastle. Deck loadings have been calculated at between 350kg/ft2 and 500kg/ft2, depending on location. Seven watertight cargo doors are arranged, and there are 13 internal ramps connecting the various decks. Electric heating is provided to the traffic lanes. The accommodation block is positioned in the three-quarter aft position, straddling the spar-deck vehicle stowage. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

AS Significant Ships reported last year, STX
Shipbuilding is a relatively new Korean shipbuilding entity, born out of the financial uncertainty which, for some time, had surrounded the former Daedong company. Although STX inherited a portfolio of successful tanker designs from Daedong, Miltiadis M represents a completely new venture for the company, and is the first vessel of this Panamax type to come from the re-organised Chinhae shipyard near
Pusan. The design has been produced following extensive investigations into hull form, propulsion performance, structural and global vibration analyses, together with considerations of optimum tank layout for this class of tanker. The result is a configuration which includes six centrally divided cargo tanks - plus two slop tanks and a residual tank, contained within a double-hull arrangement in which the double bottom is divided into three by two longitudinal divisions, and has the port and starboard tanks connected with the side tanks and serving as combined water ballast spaces. Plane transverse and centreline bulkheads present a smooth surface in the cargo tanks, which have been given a 300microns dry-film-thickness phenolic epoxy coating. Suitable for worldwide trading, Miltiadis M is a single- decked vessel without forecastle, but benefitting from a breakwater, forward on the upper deck. Access to the bow from the deckhouse is by means of a centreline gangway along the upper deck. The design complies with Det Norske Veritas Ice Class 1C rules, and is arranged to carry crude and product oils, for which purpose a cargo piping system capable of handling three segregations is fitted, using three steam turbine cargo pumps each rated at 2500m3/h. Ballasting is carried out using two 1500m3/h pumps in an interesting pairing of one steam turbine driven and one electric unit. The HSD Engine Co built the MAN B&W 6S60MC-
Mk 6 main engine which develops 12,240kW at 105rev/min. This is directly coupled to a nickel- aluminium-bronze FP propeller, and produces a sea trial speed of 15.20knots at a full deadweight of 71,522dwt (13.80m draught), with the engine running at 85% MCR and allowing a sea margin of 15%. A feature of the installation is the low level of vibration experienced in both full load and ballast conditions. Three sets of
Wärtsilä/ABB diesel alternators, each rated for an output of 960kW at 900rev/min, supply electrical requirements, and steam is produced from two 25,000kg/h oil-fired boilers. Control of the main and auxiliary engines, boilers and other machinery is effected either from the machinery control room, or from the wheelhouse, using a NABCO system. The accommodation block, built aft above the engineroom, houses 15 officers and 12 crew, together with a Suez/repair crew of six. Safety requirements are served by two sets of 36-person lifeboats handled by gravity-type davits. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

THIS new series of 'Panamax-sized' container ships has been developed to suit the needs of shippers, for whom a prime consideration has become the ability to offer customers a regular, 'fixed-day', scheduled service. This generally requires an increased speed to maintain schedules, and adequate engine power to sustain it, even in unfavourable weather conditions, and, since increasing speed inevitably has an adverse effect on stability, this aspect of the vessel's design also needed to be reconsidered. MOL Encore conforms to the now accepted configuration for 'Panamax' class container ships, but includes a number of refinements aimed at achieving the new operating objectives, with an improved hull form and a high-powered engine introduced as a means of achieving the higher speed requirement. Of particular note is the positioning of oil fuel tanks between the sandwich-type transverse hold bulkheads, instead of, as usual, in the side wing space of the double-hull. More than 50% of the total storage capacity has been moved in this transfer, which not only produces clear environmental benefits due to the safer location, but also frees additional tankage for water ballast: an essential component of container transport in both loaded and empty conditions. It also improves stability, and from this element is derived the possibility to carry two-tiers of 'high-cube' containers in the holds. Cargo is carried in eight holds, seven of which are positioned forward of the machinery room/deckhouse, with one behind this space. MacGregor pontoon covers close the hatches, and form the base for loading 2508TEU at a maximum six-tiers high on the fore deck, and seven high aft. In-hold capacity is 2070, and sockets for refrigerated boxes number 393. Cell guides are provided in the holds, and lashing bridges are fitted in way of the highest stowages _ between the seven hatches immediately forward of the bridge, and on the aft deck, to aid cargo security. The modifications to the basis design noted above have also made it possible to increase the vessel's 45ft container intake. Some oil fuel capacity is still provided in the side tanks, but in the main, these spaces are designated for ballast purposes in an arrangement which features a double bottom divided into a centre tank, and wing tanks, port and starboard which box-in the hold bilges to improve container stowage. The side wing tanks formed above these extend in height to fore and aft underdeck passageways. MOL Encore is fitted with a Sulzer 9RTA96C main engine manufactured by Diesel United. This develops 49,410kW MCR at 100rev/min, and, at 85% of that output (42,000kW/94.70rev/min), produces a speed in service of 24.50knots using a fixed-pitch propeller fitted with a Mitsui-OSK Propeller Boss Cap Fin (PBCF). The main engine is fitted with a new cylinder oil saving system based on the swirl injection principle (SIP) which provides better utilisation and considerable reduction in the consumption of cylinder oil. A Taiyo turbo-alternator supplies 1200kW to the electrical system, aided by four Yanmar/Taiyo diesel sets each developing 1820kW. Manoeuvring is assisted by a
Kawasaki 1620kW bow thruster. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

HANJIN's long success in the field of container- ship building has been well documented in
Significant Ships reviews, covering a range of vessels which has included some of the largest of this type yet built. MSC Linzie fits into a 'mid-size' position in that portfolio, and is the first unit of a new design based around the Panama Canal maximum-length parameter of 294m, at the same time complying with the
Panamax beam restriction of 32.20m. This product of the builder's own design office has already attracted more than 20 orders, with the flexibility to operate through the Canal into both Atlantic and Pacific
Oceans, carrying what is claimed to be the largest intake of containers of the many conventional
Panamax designs on the market, being a major factor in this success. MSC Linzie conforms to the now regular container ship layout, with six holds arranged within a double-hull structure. Deck cargoes are afforded protection from a forecastle, and from a breakwater just aft of this structure. Five of the holds are forward of the superstructure/machinery room, with the sixth occupying the restricted space aft, but, making use of the almost rectangular upper deck shape, offering substantial stowage on its hatch covers and above the mooring deck, for 20ft/40ft/45ft boxes. Seventeen hatches close the holds, and they are of the
MacGregor lift-on/lift-off pontoon type. Each hold is capable of accepting two stacks of high-cube containers, and is provided with cell guides suitable for 40ft units, with fittings for the stowage of 20ft equivalents. Loading on deck and hatch covers is facilitated using full-beam lashing bridges, which, as noted above, allow sizes up to 45ft to be loaded, including those containing dangerous goods. Electrical connections are provided to supply 400 reefer boxes. The main engine is an MAN B&W 9K90MC-C design, manufactured by HSD Diesel, which develops 39,780kW. When operating at 90% MCR and allowing a 15% sea margin, the trial speed at scantling draught is 23.06knots. In this sailing condition, it was favourably recorded on trials that vibration and noise levels in the accommodation spaces were noticeably reduced. The engine is directly coupled to a 8200mm diameter FP propeller, fitted in an open-water sternframe. Electrical supply is derived from four STX-MAN B&W diesel- driven, Hyundai alternators, each having an output of 1987.5kVA, and steam is produced in a composite boiler with an output of 4000/3500kg/h. A powerful 1900kW Rolls-Royce thruster is fitted at the bow. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

JUST two years ago Significant Ships of 2001 reported the entry into service of the 'biggest yet' container vessel, the 6802TEU P&O Nedlloyd Stuyvesant. Now, so quickly has box-ship sizes increased that in this 2003 review of current newbuildings, we find MSC Viviana, of similar-dimensions and container capacity as that
P&O vessel, already pushed down the size 'league table' by a vessel of 8000TEU capacity, with even further relegation imminent as a result of yet larger designs in the pipeline. MSC Viviana is a single-decked vessel with forecastle, and sunken mooring deck aft. The hull is double- skinned, with the lower side tanks formed into a 'box- shaped' bilge and joined with the outboard of three double-bottom tanks (port, starboard and centre), in all but No 2 hold. As with P&O Nedlloyd Stuyvesant, this new vessel is laid out with eight holds, however, this time only six of these are forward of the machinery space, with two arranged above the sloped aft body. Upper wing tanks abreast Nos 4, 5 and 6 holds are designated as fuel bunkers, and Nos 2 and 3 are water ballast compartments, with the latter serving additionally as port and starboard heeling tanks. The forward holds are laid out with 26 x 20ft container bays, and the aft spaces have 8 x 20ft, whilst the Hyundai 'girderless' construction system allows a maximum hold stowage arrangement of 14 rows and nine tiers, and 16/7 on deck. Ventilation to holds Nos 1 to 5, where dangerous cargoes of SOLAS Classes 1.4 to 9 can be carried, provides two air changes hourly. Total TEU capacity is calculated as 6730, with 3622 on deck and 3108 in the holds, however, the vessel is designed to accept 20ft, 40ft and 45ft containers, with the latter suitable for direct loading on to hatch covers 1A, 2A, 3A, 4A, 5A, 6A, 7A and 8A. Reefer sockets are installed for 400FEU. Cell guides sized for 40ft containers, but which are also suitable for loading seven tiers of TEU, are arranged in the cargo holds. Athwartship lashing bridges with fittings for the larger box sizes are provided in order that containers on hatch covers and upper deck stools can be conveniently lashed and secured up to four, five, or six tiers high. The MacGregor hatch covers fitted to MSC Viviana are of the lift-away pontoon type, with each cover made up of three panels, each designed to weigh less than 40tonnes to facilitate handling by shore cranes. The panels are fitted with the manufacturer's patented
Lubripad bearing pads, designed to support covers with a very low and predictable wear rate for easy maintenance, and to transfer the weight of the cover, and cargo thereon, to the structure by accommodating relative movement caused by the flexing of the hull. Key elements of a Lubripad are the balance rubber which equalises uneven loads, and a replaceable bronze bearing pad coated with low friction PFTE for smooth sliding. A Hyundai-MAN B&W 10K98MC-C main engine is fitted, developing 57,059kW MCR at 104rev/min, and running at an NCR (90% MCR) of 51,353kW/100rev/min for a service speed of 24.8knots, allowing a 15% sea margin. The four diesel alternators are also Hyundai products and each produce 2100kW. A Lips 2500kW bow thruster is fitted, and the raked-tip design of the propeller, and optimum section rudder profile, are aimed at reducing cavitation. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

NEVSKIY PROSPECT is the first delivery from a new series of Aframax tanker ordered by Russian principals, with management undertaken by Cyprus- based Unicom. The design has been prepared to
American Bureau of Shipping rules, and conforms to ice strengthening Class 1C regulations for machinery, and Class 1B, as applicable to hull scantlings. Particular features are compliance with the 'must' and 'strongly preferred' elements of the marine safety criteria for industry vessels in ExxonMobil service, and Chevron requirements for chartered vessels (voyage charter and third party vessel operating requirements). Within a double-skin hull, where the enclosed side and bottom space is utilised as water ballast tanks, a centreline, and six transverse bulkheads, form six pairs of cargo tanks and two slop tanks, arranged so that three grades of oil can be handled simultaneously, with each grade served by its own pumps and using independent suction and discharge main lines in the tanks and on deck. Three vertical centrifugal steam-turbine-driven cargo pumps rated at 3000m3/h are fitted, with control of cargo and ballast systems carried out using a conventional console in the cargo control room. Nevskiy Prospect is a single-deck vessel without forecastle, but having sheer extending forward from the divisional bulkhead between Nos 1 and 2 tanks. At the fore end, a raked stem with bulbous bow is fitted, and there is a vertical transom stern aft, surmounting a semi- balanced rudder operating in an open water sternframe with ice-knife. Accommodation for 16 officers and 14 crew is provided in a deckhouse aft, separated from the engine casing and funnel to reduce vibration and sound transmissions. The hull form incorporates ice operation characteristics and was verified at SSPA, Sweden and by
Masa-Yards' Arctic Research Centre (MARC) in Finland. A design fatigue life of 30 years has been applied to longitudinal end connections to transverse webs/bulkheads in the cargo area, in accordance with the requirements of the ABS SafeHull phase A notation. The machinery installation is based upon a MAN
B&W 5S70MC-C main engine developing 21,000bhp (15,550kW) MCR when running at 91rev/min. This is directly coupled to a FP, 8000mm diameter propeller for a service speed of 15.1knots when the engine is performing at 90% MCR. Electrical requirements are served from three alternators driven by 780kW MAN
B&W Holeby 6L23/30H diesel engines, with one emergency set also available. Steam consumption is derived from two Aalborg Mission OL boilers, each producing 25,000kg/h. Exhaust-gas emissions from the main and auxiliary diesel engines comply with IMO
NOx limits, and a low sulphur fuel oil tank is separately fitted in accordance with the requirements for SOx emission control. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

THE latest series of this shuttle tanker class is the largest yet built by Samsung, and currently numbers four vessels, including two for UNS and one for another Norwegian owner, Ludwig Mowinckel. Their dimensions are within Suezmax parameters, giving them opportunities for alternative worldwide trading; however, in deference to their principal occupation, Nordic Stavanger and her sisters comply with the specialist Norske Veritas dynamic positioning notation DYNPOS-AUTR, and the equivalent IMO
DP Class 2 standard, and are equipped with a fully redundant Kongsberg Maritime dynamic positioning installation, essential for their rendezvous with loading buoys. Nordic Stavanger will work in the Heidrun field. The DP system works in conjunction with an integrated bridge system from the same supplier, plus other navigation and communication equipment designed for station-keeping and maintaining continuous loading in significant sea states. Under the control of this specialist technology, and providing the standards of manoeuvrability required, are three powerful thruster systems supplied by Brunvoll: one 2200kW retractable thruster fitted at the bow, and another at the stern, and one tunnel thruster of the same size, also fitted forward. The propeller is of the
CP type, and the rudder is of the patented Schilling design. The ship design is that of a single-deck vessel with forecastle, and features a double-hull structure conforming to the class notation OPP-F. It is divided by a centreline, and transverse bulkheads, into six pairs of cargo tanks and two slop tanks, coated on the tank top and lower areas with tar epoxy. The surrounding side tanks, which incorporate a hopper configuration at the tank top, are common with the centrally divided double bottom tanks, and form water ballast spaces which are protected by a tar-free epoxy coating. Two cargo segregations can be carried, and these are handled by four 3000m3/h vertical centrifugal, Hamworthy KSE pumps, installed in a pumproom at the aft end of the cargo space. Two of these pumps are electrically driven and two steam driven. Arrangements are provided for loading over the bow, with a specially fashioned stem allowing the vessel to be connected to an offshore hose with minimal oil spill. A conventional diesel engine/single-screw installation has been adopted and is centred on a MAN B&W 6S70MC low-speed unit built by HSD, developing 16,860kW when running at 91rev/min. At 90% full power (15,174kW), and allowing a 15% power margin, the service speed is 14.70knots. A 1200kW shaft-driven alternator is fitted through a Renk gear, and there are four diesel-alternator sets of varying capacity available. Steam is generated by an auxiliary, and a composite boiler with outputs of 35tonnes/h and 1.6tonnes/h, respectively. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

ORIGINALLY ordered from Flensburger in 1999, commercial problems at Smyril resulted in the contract for Norröna being cancelled a year later. The contract was re-instated with Flender in 2001, but under a seperate contract, Flensburger carried out almost all the engineering work. Despite Flender Werft’s later terminal financial difficulties, the ferry was delivered to her owner's 'North-East Atlantic ferry service' in 2003. In winter, this service operates mainly as a freight link between the Faroes and Denmark, but in summer it takes on a tourism role with extensions to the Shetland Islands, Iceland and Norway. Complying with length restrictions at both the Faroese and Danish terminals; coping with severe wind and climatic problems of the region (with air conditioning performance particularly engineered to meet these arduous conditions); and matching contractual noise levels specified to be lower than DNV Comfort Rating 3 (and which have been bettered in service), were pre- requisites of the design. To reduce operating costs, public and cabin areas have been arranged to allow selective opening/closure, to suit the reduced passenger and hotel staff requirements during the winter trading season. In all, 1482 passengers can be carried, with 318, two-, three- and four-berth cabins providing 1020 beds. Couchettes serve a further 324 passengers, whilst in summer 138 persons may be carried without berths. Ro-ro traffic enters over the stern only at deck 3, using a door/ramp, with traffic moving to deck 4 by way of a tilting ramp capable of operating whilst loaded with 3 x 35tonne trucks. Between decks 4 and 5, a three-panel hoistable car deck is positioned to starboard, with one panel serving as a ramp and capable of operating under load. Lane length available for freight is 1830m and car capacity 634. The builder's hydrodynamic development of the hull form resulted in the fitting of twin, twisted, high-lift spade rudders, each incorporating Costa bulbs, suspended behind two highly skewed CP propellers. IT is almost 20 years since Samsung delivered its first container vessels: a pair of 2200TEU capacity carriers for Hapag-Lloyd. Since then, more than 155 cellular ships have left the yard, or are on order, with almost everyone keeping pace with an ever increasing, international size progression. Now, in 2003, carrying capacity has passed the 8000TEU mark, with OOCL Shenzhen, the lead ship of an 'SX' series which is currently understood to have the largest declared container ship capacity in the world. But, 'time marches on' and a higher total will be soon be carried by another Samsung class now under construction of 9200TEU, and 10,000TEU vessels are in the offing. It would be very early in the development of the container ship that its now readily identifiable configuration was established; OOCL Shenzhen, therefore, features the typical single-deck hull layout with machinery space and accommodation block positioned three- quarters aft. The latter occupies seven tiers serving a crew of 28, plus two supernumaries and a six-man Suez/repair squad. Above this, a spacious wheelhouse is equipped with three radars, two DGPS navigators and an integrated navigation system, and provides a line-of-sight over the bow which allows four tiers of containers to be stowed at the forecastle, increasing to eight at the deckhouse front. The hull is double skinned, with a tank arrangement comprising side, bilge, and three transverse double bottom compartments, devoted to water ballast and bunker capacity. Two side tanks, port and starboard, are arranged for automatic heel adjustment, with ballast water transferred across the ship by means of a heeling pump. The hatch coamings for the nine holds (eight forward of the deckhouse, one aft) are continuous and form the major longitudinal strength member. Together, the forward holds provide 15FEU bays, whilst the restricted
No 9 hold can accept three bays, with four loaded above the hatch covers and the mooring deck. The forward hold is dimensioned to carry 1 x 40ft container stack in cell guides, with 2FEU stacks loaded in cell guides in holds 2 to 8. Alternate bays on the hatch covers over holds 2 to 9 are dimensioned to accept 45ft units. Container capacity in the holds is 3715TEU, with either 4144 or 4348TEU carried on deck, depending upon whether stacks are seven or eight tiers high. Lashing bridges are fitted on deck between the hatches, and an on-deck cell guide secures containers carried above the mooring deck. Maximum stowage on deck is 17 rows of eight tiers, and in the holds it is 15 rows/nine tiers. The
MacGregor pontoon-type hatch covers each weigh 40tonnes, and are supported on Lubripad bearing pads which sustain stack loadings of 90tonnes/TEU and 120tonnes/40ft and 45ft containers, respectively. A total of 510 self-contained air-cooled refrigerated FEU can be loaded on deck, and another 200 in the hold, with dangerous goods carried in the forward holds, and on deck. OOCL Shenzen is fitted with a MAN B&W 12K98MC-
C main engine built by HSD Engine Co Ltd, developing 93,150bhp (68,520kW) at 104rev/min. When operating at 85% of this power and without the shaft alternator connected, it drives a six-bladed propeller to produce a service speed of 25knots. The shaft alternator is a Taiyo unit with an output of 3000kW, and four 3000kW
Daihatsu/Taiyo 3000kW diesel-driven sets are also provided. A 2500kW bow thruster is fitted and a SR63m rudder

GIVEN the massive enterprise which is the
Carnival Group, it is, perhaps, no surprise to find three cruise ships, owned by three of its famous subsidiary companies, featuring in this issue of
Significant Ships. If no other reason existed, the trio, Costa Fortuna, Queen Mary 2 and subject vessel Oosterdam, would still evoke interest from a comparison of the differing machinery installations selected by its respective operator. HAL (Holland America Line) has opted for a combined diesel-and-gas-turbine-driven electric power plant for this Vista class, whilst details of the alternatives fitted to the other vessels will be found in their separate reviews elsewhere. The system adopted for Oosterdam uses five Wärtsilä diesel engines: three of 11,520kW output, and two of 8640kW, to drive 3 x 11,060kW and 2 x 8300kW synchronous alternators. In addition, a 14MW General
Electric gas turbine coupled to a synchronous alternator is installed (packaged by GE group member S&S
Energy Products), and the combined output of these units provides power at 11kV 60Hz to satisfy the vessel's total electrical requirements. For ship propulsion purposes, part of this supply is directed via stepless AC/AC thyristor converters, with a rated speed of 0-160rev/min, at 15.5Hz, to two 17.6MW, synchronous, fresh-water-cooled, main propulsion/360deg azimuthing pods from ABB, positioned either side of a centreline skeg. Two oil-fired boilers each provide 12tonnes/h of saturated steam, supplemented by a supply from each of six exhaust-gas economisers. Automation and instrumentation of the system complies with Lloyd's
Register, Registro Italiano Navale, and US Coast Guard, requirements for UMS notation (unattended for 24hours), which allows automatic/remote control from bridge and engine control room, for main and auxiliary machinery. Vista-class ships have berths available for 2388 passengers and 812 crew, with the former occupying 924 cabins, ranging in size from two penthouse suites of 120m2 area to 'standard inside' rooms of 15.9m2. Balconies are arranged for 622 of these rooms. Especially provided for are the disabled, with 27 cabins available, whilst in addition, braille-equivalents of important notices are displayed for the blind. Ten automatic, 16-person passenger lifts are provided, but of particular fascination for passengers are the two panoramic lifts, postitioned externally port and starboard, and which allow fine views of the ocean below. These serve 10 decks which provide facilities such as a two-deck high show lounge forward, equipped with 'state-of-the-art' entertainment equipment, and another two-deck space aft serving as the main dining room, and featuring a two-level galley. Also of interest is the covered promenade deck which circles the vessel, and the swimming pool arranged with a retractable dome, exemplifying the list of leisure activities necessary on present-day cruise ships: others being a golf simulator, gymnasium/aerobics centre, sauna/spa, and sports deck. Other facets of everyday life presented amongst the 18 public rooms available, and which have become obligatory inclusions on this class of ship, are business centres and conference rooms, and computer facilities, whilst the usual arrangement of restaurants, bars and lounges, cafeterias, casino and night club, discos and shops are accessed from a three-deck high atrium/foyer. THE importance of the France/Corsica ferry link is emphasised by the inclusion in previous Significant
Ship reviews of three vessels, specifically built for this service. Unlike the most recent of these (Danielle
Casanova, delivered last year, and which carried some 2400 passengers), Pascal Paoli's prime function is to provide an overnight freight link between her owner's Marseilles and
Bastia terminals. At the same time, she ensures a high- quality voyage for a more modest passenger complement of only 658, berthed in 160 cabins, with 78 reclining seats also available in a lounge equipped with TV and cinema screens. This lounge forms part of a range of sitting areas and restaurants, including separate facilities for truck drivers and a play room for children, which are provided on deck 7, served by a comprehensive outfit of catering, drink supply and refrigeration equipment supplied by
MacGregor. Environmental issues have been especially considered, and an Uson Marine waste disposal plant is installed, featuring a SOLAS II-compliant chute opening at decks 7, 8 and 9, and discharging at deck 3 into 12m3 containers. Vehicles enter at deck 3 over a pair of 18.50m long stern door/ramps [1 x 12m wide (P) and 1 x 6.40m wide(S)], rated for 40tonne road trailers and 60tonne MAFI trailers. Fixed internal ramps connect with the lower garage (deck 2); the cars-only space on deck 7; and to deck 5, closed respectively by an end-hinged watertight cover, a top- hinged watertight door and a sliding door. The open area on deck 5 aft is available for the carriage of dangerous goods, and is separated from the main part of that deck by a gastight door. Decks 2, 3 and 5 offer 2300 lane metres of lorry space and 580 metres are available on deck 7 for cars. The builder's philosophy of not fitting watertight barriers in the ro-ro spaces is continued in this design. Instead, a system incorporating B5 longitudinal bulkheads in the lower garage deck, tight transverse sub-division below the main deck, a depth to the freeboard deck (No 3) of 9.80m, and a freeboard of more than 2.00m, are claimed to achieve the necessary level of damage stability. Length/draught restrictions at Bastia contributed to a hull form of high beam/draught ratio, and invoked research into vibration and noise problems which might be caused by propeller pressure pulses caused by this relationship, and the high engine power. Notable design features include the fitting of inward-turning highly- skewed twin screws, and the introduction of a semi- tunnel stern arrangement incorporating a duck-tail and trim wedge. Four Wärtsilä 9L46 main engines are provided, driving through two twin-input/single-output gearboxes fitted in a separate compartment. Each is has a PTO to a 1900kW alternator, with additional electrical supply from three 1440kW diesel alternator sets. Twin flapped rudders operate behind the propellers, and two tunnel thrusters are installed forward. Heeling tanks and a pair of ACH 17.5m2 fin stabilisers are arranged to control rolling by 90% at 20knots and 4deg wave slope. Sadly, this is one of the last ships from Van der Giessen- de Noord, which will close early this year. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

THE third Carnival Group delivery of 2003 to be reviewed in this edition of Significant Ships is the prestigious Queen Mary 2, built for the company's
Cunard Line operation which primarily requires a vessel to operate a regular six-day schedule of
Transatlantic liner crossings during the summer season, but is equally capable of serving as a cruise ship at other times. Because of this, and the rigorous weather conditions encountered in the North Atlantic, the design has been 'structure-led', with interior arrangements fitted in around strength aspects which include scantlings significantly higher than class rules, and calculated to suit a 40-year fatigue strength. The hull is designed with nine main fire zones, and 24 bulkheads dividing the length into 25 watertight compartments. The liner mode is emphasised by the outboard configuration of the vessel, which owes much to experience gained from famous predecessors on the
Southampton/New York service, and includes protection against the elements derived from a 'Normandie-style' whaleback breakwater over the forecastle, 'tiered' upperworks, and a funnel traditionally positioned about midships. Overall, the style and grandeur of the 'golden era' of 1930s Transatlantic travel has been echoed, but considered alongside present-day obligations, such as a requirement for as many cabins as possible to have balconies. Including the tanktop, Queen Mary 2 has 16 main decks and a sub- deck, with the main public areas occupying three decks, but spreading on to six more. Eight decks are allocated for passengers and there is one main crew accommodation deck, with other dedicated crew areas located on a further nine decks. The bulkhead deck (13.7m above base) has been designated Deck 1, and main passenger entry is through a central lobby at decks 2/3. These form the base of an atrium extending upwards to deck 7 and featuring two glass scenic lifts. The 4.5m tweendeck height at decks 2 and 3 allows this space to be allocated to the signature
Britannia Restaurant (one of the largest public rooms afloat), Queen's Room ballroom _ both aft of the entry lobby, and the main show lounge, the 1094-seat Royal
Court Theatre, and the Illuminations auditorium, forward. The latter is multi-functional, but particularly notable for housing the first sea-going planetarium. Casinos, games rooms, bars, lounges, a library, many eating locations, and shopping areas are located throughout the vessel, together with outside leisure and recreation spaces, and a unique and comprehensive health spa and fitness facility. Passenger cabins range in size from 209m2 'grand duplex' suites to 18m2 standard rooms. 77.6% of total cabins are classed as 'outside', including 955, which have private balconies. Thirty cabins are fully equipped for handicapped passengers and 36 more are fitted for the hard of hearing. All three of our featured Carnival ships operate on the power station electrical system, but with individual differences. Costa Fortuna uses a straight, diesel- alternator arrangement with twin conventional screws, whereas Oosterdam and Queen Mary 2 both favour
CODAG installations, with the HAL vessel driven by two azimuthing propulsion pods, and the Cunarder featuring a revolutionary arrangement of two Mermaid propulsion pods fitted each side of a central skeg, but with only the aftermost pair of the azimuthing type. The 117,200kW power plant used by this vessel is supplied from four 16,800kW Wärtsilä/ABB diesel alternator sets installed in a conventional midships engineroom, supplemented by the output from two
Brush alternators with 25,000kW General Electric gas turbine drives, positioned in a machinery room within the funnel. The diesel installation produces some 57% of power requirements, with 43% derived from the gas turbine sets. About 86,000kW is directed to propulsion purposes. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

THE remarkable progress made by STX
Shipbuilding since it emerged just two years ago as the new owner of the troubled Daedong enterprise, is demonstrated by two designs appearing in this issue of
Significant Ships. Panamax-sized Miltiadis M is a product of the new company's own design office, and her specification is already proving attractive to many owners, however, the MR range of circa-46,000dwt product tankers based on Daedong designs, of which
Resolve is an example, has achieved even greater success, with 27 vessels understood to be in service to date, and 35 more on order!
European owners have been ready customers for this class, and it is Belgian operator TransPetrol which took delivery of Resolve late in 2003: part of an order for four ships. The design, which uses less than 30% high-tensile steel in its structure, is single-decked with forecastle, and has a double hull within which there is a centreline, and six transverse, corrugated bulkheads, built on stools and forming six pairs of cargo tanks. With side stiffening included in the double hull space, and deck beams arranged externally on top of the cambered deck, the tanks present a flush internal surface which is coated with 300microns of pure epoxy. Two slop tanks and a heavy fuel bunker are positioned between the cargo tanks and the engineroom, and the side and bottom tanks in the double hull are given over to water ballast, and are coated with a light-coloured tar- free epoxy. Ballasting is handled by two Frank Mohn 750m3/h hydraulic pumps, and cargo operations use 12 x 600m3/h submerged pumps, also of Frank Mohn manufacture, to service a system which can cope with six cargo segregations. Resolve is designed to carry crude oil, oil products, and IMO Class III chemicals, with more than 100 commodities, including caustic soda, listed as suitable cargoes. For this reason, SUS316L stainless steel piping is fitted, with grade ANSI 316L used in the manufacture of the pumps. STX Shipbuilding (STX meaning System Technology
Excellence) now forms part of the Ssangyong Group, well known for its diesel engine construction, particularly of MAN B&W products under licence. The choice of an 'in-house' manufacturer for Resolve's main engine is, therefore, a reasonable one and sees the fitting of a MAN B&W 6S50MC-C Mk 7 unit; now also marketed under the STX brand-name. This is an upgrade from the 6S50MC Mk 6 versions of this engine adopted in some earlier sister ships, and produces some 900kW additional power and more than 0.50knots extra speed, although daily fuel consumption rises by 3tonnes/day. The STX engine division also supplied the
MAN B&W diesel prime movers for a trio of 740kW
Hyundai alternators which satisfy electrical requirements. Steam output comes from an 18,000kg/h auxiliary boiler and a 1100kg/h exhaust-gas economiser. Accommodation is arranged for a complement of 15 officers and 14 crew, with special provision for a six- person Suez Canal or repair crew. Life-saving facilities comprise two 35-person enclosed lifeboats carried under gravity davits. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

WHILST always ensuring that a steady base-load of tanker, bulk carrier and container ship contracts was to hand, Samsung's Geoje Shipyard has regularly taken on specialist, 'value-added' challenges, particularly in the offshore market, where changing oil production methods constantly require shipbuilders to develop new designs. Using its tanker expertise, Samsung successfully introduced shuttle-tanker derivatives such as Heidrun (Significant Ships of 1995) a few years ago, whilst, at the same time pioneering technology to suit the latest FPSO (floating production, storage and offloading) systems (Bonga, Significant Ships of 2002). For the company's lastest foray into the offshore market, Samsung has used knowledge gained from both these types of vessel to introduce the more mobile FPSO
Searose. Owner Husky Oil's original plans were for a vessel similar to some already in service, which maintained position using azimuthing units, but later, Husky accepted Samsung's novel proposal which entailed modifying an existing shuttle-tanker design to act as a basis for developing what is claimed to be a more cost- effective solution to the owner's needs. The main difference between the Husky specification and the
Samsung alternative, lies with the propelling machinery, where the proposed azimuthing units have been substituted with a more conventional system employing twin Wärtsilä 8L46B main engines and twin propellers. These are arranged to burn diesel oil, and each develops 7800kW and drives a CP propeller via a gearbox which reduces engine speed from 500rev/min to 132rev/min. Electrical requirements, including supply to three 4000m3/h cargo pumps, are served from two 2250kW diesel-alternator sets. The other interesting inclusion is a submerged turret mooring/cargo-loading system which is positioned between Nos 2 and 3 tanks, and includes nine sets of mooring winches and has 16 risers. Searose is intended for operations in the White Rose field off the eastern coast of Newfoundland - a particularly hazardous area within an iceberg zone, and it is anticipated that in the event of an iceberg warning, the machinery selection will allow the ship to disconnect the lower part of a gigantic internal monpool, which consists of positioning mooring lines and risers from the sea bed, and from the off-loading lines, and move off station at a speed of 12knots. The off-loading of processed oil to a moored shuttle tanker is carried out through a double- carcass flexible floating hose, which is run out over a hydraulically-driven reel at the stern. The hull is also strengthened to withstand a 100,000tonne iceberg. Because of its generally static role, Searose has been given a more box-shaped hull than its shuttle-tanker antecedents, but the lay-out retains the double-skin structure, within which there are six centrally-divided cargo tanks and two slop tanks, providing a total 148,000m3 of storage space, protected by a tar epoxy coating and a 10-year cathodic system using sacrificial anodes. THE perceived advantages of the azimuthing-pod system of propulsion, as applied to cruise ship design, were first described to Significant Ships readers in 1998, in the review of Carnival Cruise Line's Elation, and have been featured again in a number of issues since then. Despite teething troubles with some of the designs currently available, the concept continues to attract interest and has now been adopted for Seven Seas
Voyager. This vessel is especially noteworthy as the first recipient of another variant of the system, conceived by two newcomers to this type of technology (although both are otherwise well established in the marine field):
German electronics group STN Atlas, and Wärtsilä
Propulsion (formerly John Crane Lips), who have collaborated in the development of a product marketed under the name Dolphin. Whilst the general principles of podded propulsion are the same for all manufacturers, engineering differences of the various systems do apply, partly in respect of control characteristics of the electric propulsion motors, where, for example, cyclo- converters and permanently excited motors can be used. STN/Wärtsilä, however, has adopted synchro- converters for this aspect of its system. For Seven Seas
Voyager, the two hydrodynamically optimised Dolphin azimuthing pods, which are claimed to present a more compact unit using fewer components than those of competitors, have a continuous rotating angle of 360deg, and each contains an air-cooled, electric motor which delivers 7000kW at 172rev/min. These are supplied from a diesel-electric power- station installation based on four VEM 7200kVA/600rev/min alternators, each fitted with
Wärtsilä 9L38 prime movers each developing 5940kW at 600rev/min. This also satisfies the ship's basic electrical requirements, and forms part of a package supplied by STN Atlas containing an extensive range of electronic controls and navigation equipment. Two 1200kW Lips 'super-silent' bow thrusters operating at 263rev/min are also included. A feature of this vessel's design is its claim to be only the second cruise ship to have an 'all-balcony' arrangement of cabins. In addition, by virtue of its machinery arrangement, it has been possible to access all of these from central avenues on each passenger deck, rather than port and starboard passages. Cabin sizes range from 33m2 area to two master suites of 1390m2, with many arrangements allowing connecting suites and triples. Marble has been extensively used in bathroom design, and elsewhere on the ship, with wood finishes also a particular feature. What must be a novel inclusion on a five-star cruiseship is the provision of self-service launderettes on the passenger decks, fully equipped with washing machines, dryers, and ironing boards! Seven Seas Voyager is of a similar design to the earlier, Chantiers de l'Atlantique-built Seven Seas Mariner, and her internal arrangements owe much to customer feedback from that vessel. Decor, location of facilities such as casino, spa, and dining rooms, have all benefitted from such comments. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

THE expertise accumulated by Kawasaki over many years of building gas-carrying vessels has been noted in previous issues of Significant Ships. Those vessels so described have, in the main, been ocean- going transports equal in size to the largest of the type yet built. Shinju Maru No 1, however, is very much at the lower end of the capacity scale, and was specifically designed to carry small quantities of LNG within
Japan's domestic fuel distribution market. The vessel will operate principally on the relatively short haul between the Tobata terminal on the southern island of
Kyushu, to Takamatsu (on Shikoku island) and
Okayama (on Honshu), satisfying the needs of public utilities in this region, many of whom are planning to increase the heat value of city gas by adding LNG. Construction of the vessel has been subcontracted by
Kawasaki to Higaki Shipbuilding. It is claimed to be the first LNG carrier of this size built for the coastal market, and differs from the conventional, larger types mentioned above, by having cargo tanks constructed in accordance with Kawasaki's new 'pressure build-up' concept. Disposal of boil-off gases generated during voyages can be a major concern on large LNG tankers, but with this system, these gases are totally contained within the tanks, with the heat generated retained in the cargo. Hence, with no 'boil-off gas' to be dispersed, it is no longer necessary to have a main engine installation with a facility to burn the surplus. As a result, it has been possible to fit Shinju Maru No 1 with a diesel- powered installation, making operation now economically comparable with that of the pressurised
LPG carriers which have been working in the Japanese domestic distribution trades for some time now. Despite its immediate intended occupation, Shinju
Maru No 1 is built with scantlings and facilities suitable for worldwide trading. Basically, she is designed with a single deck, a 'double-height' forecastle, and a poop. Two cargo holds are arranged, with a wide double-hull extended at the bilge into large hopper side tanks whose structure forms part of the support for the single Type
C horizontal cylindrical tank fitted into each. These are constructed of aluminium alloy and are suitable for a pressure of 3bar and a temperature of -163oC. Almost half the diameter of the tanks extends above the top of the hold and is protected by a fore-and-aft trunk, built level with the forecastle at the bow, and ending just short of the deckhouse aft. Insulation is by means of the proven Kawasaki panel system which maintains the gas in almost the same condition as when it was loaded - even after several days at sea. Cargo is handled by two Shinko submerged centrifugal pumps, each of 300m3/h duty, fitted in each tank. The nature of the vessel's regular employment on short voyages results in frequent berthing and manoeuvring, and for this reason the main propulsion installation, based on a Hanshin LH36LA diesel engine driving a CP propeller, is assisted by a 49kN bow thruster and a Schilling Monovec high-lift rudder. The engine develops 1912kW at 270rev/min, and at 85% of that output a service speed of 12.7knots is achieved. Electrical supply is derived from two Yanmar/Taiyo diesel-driven alternator sets, each having an output of 320kW. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

OSHIMA's expertise in the world of bulk carrier design and construction is now considered by the yard to be sufficient to warrant the claim made in its publicity that the company is 'the bulker builder'. References cover many innovative vessels of this type, with a particular speciality being the 'open-hatch, general cargo/container carrier' which first appeared in the company's portfolio a decade ago. Vessels were built for operation worldwide by a consortium of
Norwegian and Japanese owners (Saga Spray, Significant
Ships of 1994). Star Oshimana is the lead ship of the latest series of this class, which now, by virtue of modest increases in dimensions above those earlier units, is believed to include the biggest deadweight capacity of such vessels using the Panama Canal. The main feature of this design is the cargo handling installation, which comprises a twin, electric, Kone
Munckloader gantry crane arrangement; this runs along tracks at the hatch sides, straddling the 11 cargo holds, which are closed by weathertight pontoons handled by the cranes. Two sets of non-watertight pontoons are also provided, and these can be fitted in each of holds 4 and 8 to form tweendecks suitable for a loading of 5.5tonnes/m2. Stowage for the panels is arranged in number 11 hold when not in use. The cranes are computer-controlled and are fitted with athwartship- traversing trolleys; each has a lifting capacity of 68tonnes. They can be turned through ±190deg and operated over the ship's side P&S on extending arms. A fixed roof over the gantries provides rain protection whilst working cargo, with retractable covers for the arms performing the same duty over the quayside. The double-hull structure forms combined L-shaped side and bottom tanks divided by a duct keel at the centreline. They enclose virtually box-shaped holds with flush surfaces, suitable for a wide variety of bulk and packaged cargoes. Containers can also be loaded, with a total TEU capacity in the holds and on deck of 2286 (including 22 refrigerated units). TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

TA-HO is the first self-unloading cement carrier of this size to be designed and built by CSBC, and, in addition to being of note as a result of the loading and unloading equipment installed to handle this cargo, the vessel is of particular interest for the choice of propelling machinery which has been made. This comprises two Kawasaki azimuthing propulsion units positioned right aft, either side of the centreline. Each is fitted with a CP propeller operating in a Kort nozzle, and is turned by a Taiyo 2000kW/1170rev/min electric motor through a Z-drive. Electrical supply for these units, cargo-handling equipment, and normal ship's services, is obtained from three Daihatsu/Taiyo 1600kW, and a single 800kW, diesel-alternator sets located in the machinery space. The propulsion units are designed to be steerable through 360deg, with manoeuvrability enhanced by a 650kW bow thruster, and, when operating at design draught, 81.6% MCR and with a 15% sea margin, the service speed is 12.50knots. The vessel is of single-deck design with forecastle, raked stem (no bulbous bow), central skeg aft, and transom stern. There are four cargo holds within a double-skin hull, served by mechanical and pneumatic loading/unloading systems supplied by H W Carlsen
AB. The hold bottoms are arranged with aeration panels inclined towards the centres of the holds at a minimum 9deg to the horizontal in all directions. Air is passed through these to fluidise the cement which then flows to a central trunk to be conveyed by compressed air to the deck for unloading. The space under the panels is arranged as a void for easy maintenance. Two airslides are installed on deck for mechanical loading at a rate of 1200tonnes/h, with unloading by this means accomplished at a rate of 600tonnes/h. Using the pneumatic loading/discharge facilities, duties are 1000tonnes/h and 600tonnes/h respectively. A five-tier deckhouse is arranged aft and provides accommodation for a complement of 10 officers and 11 crew, all in single rooms. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

THIS newly developed CSBC design is described by the builder as an 'ultra-large Panamax bulk carrier' which, it is claimed, will set a new market benchmark for this type of vessel, by having the largest cargo hold capacity and deadweight amongst Panamax fleets. In addition, an optimal hull-form, developed with the aid of computational fluid dynamics (CFD), and rigorously tested at the Hamburg Ship Model Basin (HSVA), in association with a large-diameter propeller, not only gives a superior propulsion performance, but adds low fuel consumption to the benefits on offer with this design. China Shipbuilding has resisted the trend towards a double-skin configuration for this series, opting instead for a seven-hold layout which complies with the
American Bureau of Shipping SafeHull notation. The structure contains some 70% high-tensile steel, and includes reinforcement for the carriage of iron ore in holds 1, 3, 5 and 7; for sailing with numbers 2, 4 and 6 empty; and for No 4 hold to be available for use as a water ballast tank on heavy weather ballast voyages. The ballast tanks are designed to comply with IMO regulations relating to ballasting at sea, and the latest
IACS unified requirements S26, S27 and S30 are fulfilled. A water ingress alarm system serves the cargo holds, satisfying the new SOLAS rules. Another claim made for this design is that the length/width of hatches covering Nos 2 to 7 holds (17.10m x 15.03m) provides openings which are the largest amongst vessels of this type, thereby providing operators with easier cargo handling and faster loading/unloading times. Tsuji side-rolling covers are fitted to all cargo hatches, and these are driven by chains, with manually operated cleating. Tai Progress is fitted with a Mitsui-built MAN B&W 5S60MC-C main engine, which is derated to produce 9996kW MCR at 93.5rev/min and 8158kW NCR (81.6% MCR) at 87.4rev/min. The propeller is fixed pitch, with a diameter of 7050mm, producing a service speed at design draught and NCR output of 14.5knots, allowing a 15% sea margin. MARPOL NOx emission control requirements for the main and auxiliary engines, and for the incinerator, have been complied with. Electrical supply is derived from three
Daihatsu/Nishishiba diesel-driven alternator sets, each rated at 440kW and featuring Daihatsu’s new DC-17 engine model; and steam is produced from a vertical composite boiler with an output of 1500kg/h, serving, amongst other uses, the air conditioning system installed in the five-tier-high deckhouse aft, which houses a complement of 10 officers and 13 crew. WHILST this first vessel in a new series of container ships developed by Jurong Shipyard is typical of many similar-sized designs currently available worldwide, its builder does claim certain benefits for its product. These result mainly from intensive model testing of the hull form, undertaken with the aim of producing a ship with a high service speed, coupled with a high intake of containers, each homogeneously loaded in accordance with the IMO stability criteria of 14tonnes. The outcome of these technical deliberations is a vessel which can take on board almost 2000TEU loaded to this standard, and transport them at a speed in excess of 22knots at scantling draught, with the engine running at 90% MCR and allowing a 15% sea margin. For this purpose, a MAN B&W 7K80MC-C main engine has been installed, developing 25,270kW when running at 104rev/min and driving a 7190mm diameter, five-bladed, FP propeller. High-powered container ships are often prone to vibration problems and overcoming these was a prime consideration of the hull optimisation process, which included a finite-element analysis. Investigation of the wake-field reduced propeller-induced vibration as a result of improvements made to the propeller’s design and skewing. Other aspects of the machinery installation include an electrical supply from four Wärtsilä/Leroy Somer diesel-alternator sets each producing 1290kW at 900rev/min, and steam generation by a composite boiler with an output of 2500kg/h from the oil-fired section and 3000kg/h from the exhaust-gas side. A
Kawasaki bow thruster fitted with a 1200kW motor is installed and develops a thrust of 16tonnes. Thomas Mann is of double-skin configuration with single deck and machinery/accommodation arranged three-quarters aft. There is no cargo hold aft of this position but container stacks can be loaded on the main deck. No 1 hold is accessed through an extended forecastle, afforded protection from a breakwater. The
MacGregor hatch covers have been manufactured in the shipyard and are of the non-sequential pontoon type. TEU stack loading in the holds is 150tonnes, and on No 1 hatch cover 60tonnes, increased to 70tonnes on covers 2-10. For FEU the values are 180/90/100tonnes, respectively. Reefer connections are provided for 96TEU in the holds and 304 on deck. Whilst Thomas Mann has been delivered in 'gearless' mode, provision is made for the design to incorporate four 40tonne container-handling cranes. A further option, included in the third vessel in the series, is the fitting of lashing bridges, whilst the possibility exists for hatch coamings to be raised to accommodate two high-cube units, instead of one, in the holds. KNOWN to the shipyard as the Ro-Ro 3900 type, the design of Tor Magnolia is a derivative of that created for Turkish operator UND (UND Akdenis -
Significant Ships of 2000), and later adapted to suit a series of vessels built for a British Ministry of Defence sea lift-charter (Hurst Point - Significant Ships of 2002). This is especially notable for hull form and propulsion developments, conceived both 'in house' and in conjunction with research facilities at local universities and the HSVA tank, which resulted in an unusual choice of machinery installation for this class of vessel - a slow-speed diesel engine driving a single screw. This utilises a nine-cylinder example of the MAN B&W
L60MC-C engine (a recent extension to a series previously limited to eight cylinders) which produces 20,070kW, and when operating at 90% MCR with a 15% sea margin, can give the ship a service speed of 22.8knots. It is claimed that this 'slow-running-engine/single- screw' concept, ensures for Tor Magnolia performances which are also attributable to a hull form which incorporates a single-skeg in the aft body. The main features of this arrangement are reduced wave patterns, an even wake-field, and excellent water flow to the propeller which it produces. The underwater lines of this hull form are actually shaped into a soft tunnel beneath the transom, introduced to accommodate the 6100mm diameter CP propeller. Also of interest is the fitting of a new asymmetric- section rudder. Developed by Flensburger, this is a single-piece assembly in which the leading edge is twisted to gain maximum advantage from the rotational energy of the water behind the propeller. Additionally, the rudder incorporates a Costa bulb, today a standard feature with Flensburger designs. A KGW ram-type steering gear operates the rudder, which can be turned through 45deg port and starboard. Assisting its manoeuvring qualities are twin thrusters, forward and aft, the former developing 1500kW for a thrust of 150kN each, whilst the latter, of 900kW, each produce 123kN. The MAN B&W main engine referred to above is fitted in a machinery compartment positioned about three-quarters aft, with electric power supplied from four 1520kW MAN B&W Holeby 7L21/31 diesel- alternator sets, fitted with catalytic converters. Two boilers, of 3500kg/h and 2500kg/h output, satisfy steam requirements. A combined Rolls-Royce Intering anti-heeling and roll stabilisation system is fitted, and this is designed to cope with the various loading conditions expected to arise in service. The Ro-Ro 3900 design has a double-skin hull above main deck level, and side ballast tanks below. Four fixed vehicle decks (tank top, main, upper and weather) are arranged, with two hoistable vehicle decks available forward of the engine casing, and providing space for an additional 300 cars when in use. Vehicle access is over the stern at main deck level by way of a single 18.10m wide x 14m long ramp, supplemented by 4.3m wide x 4m hinged side doors forward, port and starboard. Links with the upper deck are provided by a fixed 4m wide internal ramp, and a 4m wide x 61.5m long hoistable ramp, which is in direct line with another fixed ramp taking vehicles up to the weather deck. Direct access to the upper deck is also possible using an 8m wide shore linkspan over the stern, and the tanktop is reached from the main deck by a fixed ramp. A clear height above the maindeck allows double- stack MAFI trailers to be loaded and all the vehicle decks are flush, and fitted with SAT lashing pots that secure trailer trestles using hydraulic twistlocks. Ventilation openings are arranged in the ship's side between upper and weather decks, and a forecastle whaleback affords protection for cargo stowed on these decks. Refrigerated cargo is catered for by the provision of 90 electrical sockets. Accommodation for 17 officers and crew, and for 18 passengers in 12 cabins, is in a two-tier-high plus wheelhouse, superstructure forming a bridge over the otherwise open weather deck. The wheelhouse extends over the full beam of the ship, giving all-round visibility, and is fully equipped as a Ship Control Centre including integrated bridge and electronic sea chart (ECDIS) facilities. STATE-OWNED Admiralty Shipyards, in St
Petersburg, will be celebrating the 300th anniversary of its founding during next year (2004) and highlighting a history spent mainly constructing naval tonnage. Recently, there has been increased involvement with merchant ship construction, with tankers designed for Arctic service a feature of the programme (see Astrakhan, Significant Ships of 2000). This new series of high-specification Panamax-class products tanker, however, introduced with Troitsky
Bridge, is aimed towards more conventional trading in worldwide markets. They are in fact modified and upgraded versions of a project originally conceived by the Trogir yard in Croatia for Laurin Maritime. The design features a double-hull structure enclosing a cargo space divided by a vertically troughed centreline, and five transverse bulkheads, into five pairs of cargo tanks, and two slop tanks. With side stiffening within the double hull, and deck beams arranged externally, the tanks present a smooth surface, which has been given a
Jotun Tankguard high-build coating, suitable for handling five grades of oil and petroleum products, and
IMO Type III chemicals, simultaneously. These can be loaded or discharged using 10 Frank
Mohn, hydraulically operated, submerged pumps, each of 550m3/h capacity. Two similar, 200m3/h units serve the slop tanks. Working together, the cargo pumps can discharge a full cargo in 16hours, with loading effected through all five manifolds in 12hours, or, using one manifold connection, in 60hours. Control of the system, and the cargo pumps is carried out from a central cargo control room. Cargo heating uses a deck-mounted, stainless steel, steam heater for each tank, and cargo piping is fabricated from mild steel. An inert-gas system with a minimum capacity of 500m3/h is provided, and a single-nozzle, programmable, crude oil type, tank- washing machine is installed in each tank. A hot sea- water washing line is also fitted using sea water heated to 60o
C in a slop tank, and delivered to a cleaning machine in each tank. A cold water washing system is also provided. A venting system fitted with high-speed pressure/vacuum valves and gas-freeing facilities is installed, together with vapour emission control in accordance with IMO and US federal regulations. Gas detection sensors are fitted in ballast tanks and void spaces, and a remote sounding system serves the cargo and slop tanks, and includes high level and overfill alarms, and temperature sensors. The main engine is a Bryansk-built MAN B&W 6S50MC-C unit with a MCR of 8310kW at 123rev/min. At 90% of this rating, a service speed of 15knots is obtained from a four-bladed FP propeller. For electrical supply, three diesel-driven alternator sets are installed, two with Wärtsilä 8L20 prime movers producing 1600kVA each, and a third fitted with a 4L20 engine developing 850kVA. All the machines drive A
Van Kaick alternators. An auxiliary oil-fired boiler generates 19tonnes of steam hourly, with an exhaust- gas boiler supplying 1.6tonnes/h. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS
