LINDENAU Shipyard has frequently appeared in
Significant Ships reviews as designer and builder of a range of medium-sized safety tankers. On this occasion, however, the company enters as designer- only of a new series of vessel, introduced in conjunction with German procurement contractor
Ferrostahl, and Dutch/Romanian builder Damen
Galatz. Designated 'SCOT' (Safety Chemical Oil
Tanker), the concept is based on the fact that many of the worst tanker disasters recorded have come about from stranding after a mechanical failure. To counter this problem, the new design features a machinery installation with two main engines fitted in completely separate rooms, and has twin screws, rudders, and steering gears. Even with failure of any of these items, the vessel can, it is argued, still be operated using the second system. Within a double-skin hull, the cargo space is divided into seven pairs of cargo tanks and two slop tanks, ranging in capacity between 310m3 and 1185m3, by a centreline, and seven transverse, corrugated bulkheads. With all stiffening arranged outside the tanks, the interior presents a plain surface which is protected with
Phenguard epoxy coating. The centreline bulkhead is extended through into the machinery space, dividing it into two enginerooms, each fitted with an MAN B&W
Alpha 6L27/38 main engine developing 1800kW at 750rev/min, together with relevant auxiliaries. As part of a package from MAN B&W's Alpha works at Frederikshavn, Denmark, each engine drives a CP propeller through a reduction gearbox, to which is connected a 525/473kVA alternator. Three 473kVA diesel-alternator sets are also fitted, and the electrical supply operates through duplicated main switchboards, installed in separate compartments outside the enginerooms, forward of the machinery control room. A 400kW bow thruster is fitted, enhancing manoeuvrability derived from the twin rudders controlled from two independent steering gear compartments, arranged right-aft, as part of an installation which has been awarded the additional
Gemanischer Lloyd notation RP3 50%, covering propulsion redundancy. A feature of the machinery installation is the reduction in NOx emissions to values well below IMO demands. In line with contemporay thinking, a 'black- box' data recorder is installed and technical operation of the ship whilst in service can be overseen from a shore base by satellite communication. Wappen von Hamburg and sister vessels comply with
Exxon minimum safety criteria, and are classified as
IMO Type 2 tankers, with their particular high profile tank coating suitable for the carriage of over 500 liquids. These are handled by 14 (two per tank) Frank
Mohn 200m3/h submerged cargo pumps, with two 300m3/h units fitted in the slop tanks. Five portable pumps are also available to handle molasses, and one spare 70m3/h unit is also carried. All cargo tanks can be heated to 80oC, and loading/unloading, including handling over the stern using a manifold arranged aft on the port side, is accomplished under computer control. THIS medium-sized chemical/products tanker has played an interesting part in Sedef's development, since it provided the company with an important tanker reference from which to expand its interests in the international market. Designed for world-wide trading with IMO II cargoes, the vessel is currently operating in the Northern Europe sector. Within a double-hull structure, there are six pairs of cargo tanks of varying sizes, formed from a centreline, and transverse bulkheads of troughed steel construction. Tank internals are coated with Hempel's phenolic epoxy, and all piping plus the cargo pumps is made from stainless steel. Cargo handling is controlled from a station on the bridge deck, port side, affording the operator a good view of proceedings. Frank Mohn hydraulic submerged pumps are fitted to each cargo tank, and to the two slop tanks positioned between the cargo space and the engineroom. They operate through a cargo manifold on the upper deck, capable of delivering eight segregations. A computerised Saab TankRadar system provides cargo control, monitoring and alarms, and includes control of pumps and valves, as well as simultaneously carrying out load calculations. Cargo heating utilises two thermal oil systems, the primary, working between 150oC and 220oC, and a secondary system between 160oC and 180oC. Within the tanks, double heating coils of 316L grade stainless steel are used, and remote controlled, double-acting, Westad valves with Danfoss rotary actuators are fitted in the cargo pipelines. Separate 95% and 98% 'hi-hi' level alarms operate in each tank. Tank washing is provided, and gas freeing using water-driven portable ventilation fans is available. A 12,000m3/h gas drying unit is located in the forecastle. The design does not incorporate a forecastle, however, a poop erection is provided and, together with a two-tier deckhouse, accommodates six officers and 10 crew in single cabins arranged with private modular toilets. The double-duct air conditioning system is designed to suit an inside winter environment of 24oC, 50% relative humidity, outside -25oC; and summer conditions inside 29oC, 50% relative humidity, outside 35oC, 70% relative humidity. Above the accommodation decks is the wheelhouse, offering excellent visibility from the navigation and bridge control consoles. As noted above, cargo control is also effected from this position. The machinery installation qualifies for an unattended notation and uses a Lyngsø automation system to control and monitor operations. The main engine is a
MaK 6M32C design, having an MCR of 2800kW at 600rev/min and delivering a service speed of 13.10knots at 85% of this output. The drive to the CP propeller is through a Reintjes reduction gearbox which has a PTO to a 630kW alternator. Three 480kW diesel- driven sets are also fitted, and, in common with the rest of the installation, they are fresh-water-cooled in association with heat exchangers. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS

WHEREAS the use of train ferries is reducing in many countries, to be replaced by bridges and tunnels, transport economics in China are such that this mode of freight and passenger movement continues to play an important part in the country's development, with at least two major projects, one in the north and one in the south, currently underway. It is for the latter that Yue Hai Tie 1 Hao was delivered by its Shanghai- based builder late in 2002 (too late for inclusion in our review of that year), and, after extensive trials and tests, entered service on the 12.5-mile crossing of the
Qiongzhou Strait, between Haikou and Hainan Island: a journey of some 50 minutes duration. The design of the vessel was undertaken by MARIC, and one important aspect of that process was to give consideration to the effects of a significant tidal range in the strait. At the same time, British access-system specialist
Transmarine carried out design investigations into the arrangement of linkspan connections at the two terminals, suitable for the loading of railway carriages and wagons over the stern on to the main deck of the vessel, where four rail tracks are available in a space divided by a central casing. Loading capacity is 40 x 80tonne rail wagons, or 18 passenger carriages each weighing 18tonnes. Because of the short voyage in relatively sheltered waters undertaken by the vessel, this deck is open at the aft end, and there are also a series of side openings serving access passageways at the top of the cargo space, port and starboard. The open upper deck, forward of the superstructure, is available for the carriage of 50 lorries, and 40 cars can also be accommodated. These vehicles drive directly on to the deck over shore ramps at entries towards the bow on the port side, and three-quarters aft on the starboard side. Because the rail track is flush with the deck, additional road vehicles can be carried on the main deck if required. Passenger accommodation is minimal; however, seating, and VIP cabins, is available in the superstructure for 630 out of a total 1360 travellers, with promenade space also provided. Access for passengers is over shore-based ramps to the upper deck aft quarter, port and starboard. Lifesaving equipment fitted includes a Viking marine evacuation system, port and starboard. The propulsion machinery consists of two Wärtsilä 6L32 main engines, each developing 2880kW at 750rev/min, and driving CP propellers at 173.6rev/min through Wärtsilä reduction gearboxes for a service speed in Beaufort 2 conditions, 90% MCR and allowing 10% sea margin, of 15knots. This performance also assumes that the two gearbox-driven 1280kW alternators are disengaged. TECHNICAL PARTICULARS
